# Digital rock and pore network modelling

> 229 SCA symposium papers in this topic, most central in the citation graph (PageRank) first. Topic keywords: pore, permeability, digital, network, images, porosity, image, model, flow, size.

TL;DRs, summaries, keywords and method labels were written by Claude (Sonnet 5.5) from each paper's text and can contain errors; titles, authors and years come from the papers themselves. Check the PDF before relying on a detail. Index of all topics: https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/llms.txt

## Automated Mineral Identification of Sandstone Samples Using SEM/Image Analysis Techniques

- ID: SCA1991-09EURO, year: 1991
- Authors: Theo W. Fens, Jos H.M. Bonnie, William D. Clelland
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: SEM/EDX, backscattered electron imaging, automated mineralogy, image analysis, pseudo elemental ratios, matrix acidising
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1991/1-SCA1991-09EURO.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1991-09EURO)

An automated SEM/EDX/image-analysis method quantifies mineralogy, grain texture and pore structure in epoxy-impregnated sandstone, cutting unclassified objects from 8% to under 1% and guiding acid selection.

Conventional thin-section point counting is subjective and semi-quantitative, so the authors built a fully automated SEM/EDX/image-analysis system for sandstone petrography. Epoxy-impregnated, automatically polished samples are imaged in BSE (512x512 pixels, 1.8x1.8 mm field), grains are segmented by morphological operations, and each grain is scanned for X-rays in nine elemental windows. Classification uses pseudo elemental ratios (0/1/2) against reference minerals, cutting analysis time per grain from 10 s to 3 s, with a second vector-comparison algorithm for clays and lithics. Unclassified objects fell from about 8% to under 1%, at the cost of about one hour per field of view containing 500-800 objects. Examples include arkosic and shaly sandstones and an acidisation study where regular mud acid dissolved most calcite cement and risked matrix collapse, while half-strength mud acid dissolved it only partly and raised porosity and permeability.

## Improved Pore Network Extraction Methods

- ID: SCA2005-20, year: 2005
- Authors: A.P. Sheppard, R. M. Sok, H. Averdunk, Applied Mathematics
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: pore network extraction, medial axis transform, pore merging, micro-CT, percolation threshold, homotopic thinning
- Cited by 5 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-20.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-20)

A parallel medial-axis pore network extraction method with topology-preserving, dynamically updated pore merging is applied to micro-CT images of two sandstones and a limestone, giving robust networks.

Pore network extraction from micro-CT images is sensitive to noise and to how adjacent nodes are merged into pores. The authors build networks on the medial axis, found by distance-ordered homotopic thinning, so the network stays homeomorphic to the pore space. They add a topology-preserving merging algorithm with dynamically updated throat quality, which avoids snowballing, and a watershed partition into pores and throats. It was applied to 600^3 subsets of 2000^3 micro-CT images of two sandstones and a limestone, with low, mid and high segmentations and merge/no-merge variants. Networks were robust to segmentation and merging parameters. Sandstone-1 had a mean coordination number of only 4.3 but many 3-rings and a high site percolation threshold of about 0.47-0.50, and the limestone had a much larger critical radius.

## A Lattice-Boltzmann Based Method Applied to Digital Rock Characterization of Perforation Tunnel Damage

- ID: SCA2016-058, year: 2016
- Authors: Bernd Crouse, David M Freed, Nils Koliha, Gana Balasubramanian, Derek Bale, Stephen Zuklic
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: lattice-Boltzmann method, perforation damage, micro-CT digital rock, surfel boundary condition, absolute permeability, Berea sandstone
- Cited by 7 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-058.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-058)

A surfel-boundary lattice-Boltzmann method accurately predicts permeability at coarse resolution and is used to map porosity and permeability damage around a perforation tunnel in Berea sandstone.

Lattice-Boltzmann pore-scale simulation needs accurate wall boundary treatment when pores are resolved by few grid cells. The authors use a surfel-based (planar facet) boundary scheme and test it on flow through contacting cubic-centered packed spheres, matching the Kozeny-Carman reference permeability when the inscribed pore radius spans at least one grid cell. They then apply it to a 3 mm by 12 mm Berea plug from an API RP19B Section IV perforation test, imaged by micro-CT at 2.4 µm in four overlapping scans. The damage zone extends about 10 mm from the tunnel; porosity rises from 9% to 12% into native rock while permeability rises from 30 to 160 mD. Damaged rock has fewer large pores and more small pores, and the most frequent pore radius is only 2-3 grid cells.

## Quantitative 3D Characterisation of the Pore Space of Real Rocks: Improved µ-CT Resolution and Pore Extraction Methodology

- ID: SCA2007_17, year: 2007
- Authors: S. Youssef, E. Rosenberg, N. Gland, S. Bekri, O. Vizika
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: micro-CT, pore network extraction, skeletonization, mercury intrusion simulation, Fontainebleau sandstone, carbonate microporosity
- Cited by 7 papers in the corpus, cites 9
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_17.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_17)

A lab micro-CT and skeleton-based pore partitioning workflow extracts pore networks, simulating mercury intrusion and permeability that match experiments in sandstones but not heterogeneous carbonates.

Pore network models need accurate pore-space geometry and topology; the authors combine a lab micro-CT (Nanotom, 3 µm voxels, ~2.5 h acquisition, resolution better than 2 µm) with an in-house pore-space partitioning algorithm. The workflow involves binarisation, skeleton extraction (distance-ordered homotopic thinning), partitioning into pores and throats, and parameter extraction. Mercury intrusion and absolute permeability were simulated on the extracted networks and compared with experiments on four Fontainebleau sandstones (6.5–21.6% porosity) and two carbonates (Estaillades, Brauvilliers). Good agreement was obtained for the sandstones and Estaillades (simulated 80 mD vs 130 mD measured), while the lowest-porosity Fontainebleau sample required 3 µm resolution to give a percolating cluster (18 mD vs 0.3 mD at 6 µm). Brauvilliers failed to give a percolating cluster or permeability, because of multi-scale heterogeneity, microporosity and a non-representative volume (CT porosity 16.8% vs 32% on the plug).

## Direct Hydrodynamic Simulation of Multiphase Flow in Porous Rock

- ID: SCA2013-014, year: 2013
- Authors: D. Koroteev, O. Dinariev, N. Evseev, D. Klemin, S. Safonov, S. Berg, C. van Kruijsdijk, R. Armstrong, M. T. Myers, L. Hathon, H. de Jong
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: direct hydrodynamic simulation, diffuse interface, capillary desaturation curve, pore-filling events, synchrotron micro-CT, digital rock
- Cited by 4 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2013/SCA2013-014.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2013-014)

A GPU-based diffuse-interface pore-scale simulator (DHD) is validated against classical pore-scale displacement, micromodel, core-flood and synchrotron micro-CT data, showing good agreement for two-phase flow.

The paper presents Direct Hydrodynamic (DHD) simulation, a density-functional, diffuse-interface pore-scale simulator running on GPU clusters, for digital rock multiphase flow. It is validated on Lenormand piston-type and meniscus-collapse events, Roof snap-off criteria, and pore-doublet imbibition. Dynamics are tested against high-speed micromodel drainage, where the ~12 ms event time scale is reproduced. At the macroscale, a digitized water-wet sandstone (porosity 0.23, 1150 mD) gives a capillary desaturation curve in very good agreement with literature, and relative permeability shows capillary-number trends like core-flood data. Simulated pore-filling events from synchrotron micro-CT of Berea sandstone match experiment closely, e.g. 5.92 nL vs 5.9 nL displaced volume. Single-phase permeability and porosity matched within about 15% and 10%.

## Large two phase Digital Rock Physics simulations for relative permeability uncertainty assessment

- ID: SCA2021-007, year: 2021
- Authors: Mohamed Regaieg, Igor Bondino, Clément Varloteaux, Titly Farhana Faisal, Jianhui Yang, Richard Rivenq
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network modelling, relative permeability, film flow oil-wet, network stitching, finite size effect, residual oil saturation
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2021/SCA2021-007.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2021-007)

A parallelized pore-network workflow with stitched extraction and a revised film-flow model simulates plug-scale images, matches microplug and SCAL relative permeability, and shows finite-size effects on Sor.

The paper presents a digital rock workflow combining GNextract network extraction (Stokes conductances from OpenFOAM), a stitching algorithm for large images, and a parallelized quasi-static simulator, DynaPNM. A revised film-flow assumption, which disconnects water layers in oil-wet pores, makes simulated Krw trends follow Craig's rules, with higher Krw and lower Kro in oil-wet than water-wet cases. Statistical uncertainty runs (thousands of realizations, P10/P50/P90) constrained by Sor from images were validated against three microplug experiments and one SCAL dataset on Reservoir Sandstone B, with satisfactory agreement. A Bentheimer network of 63 million elements (8480x8480x10000 voxels, 46 cm3) ran in 6.5 hours on 81 CPUs, about two orders of magnitude faster than sequential. Sor dispersion decreased with larger image size, with images around 1200^3 dominated by finite-size effects.

## A Comparative Study of Digital Rock Physics and Laboratory SCAL Evaluations of Carbonate Cores

- ID: SCA2010-24, year: 2010
- Authors: A. Grader, M.Z. Kalam, J. Toelke, Y. Mu, N. Derzhi, C. Baldwin, M. Armbruster, T. Al Dayyani, A. Clark, G. Bin-Dhaaer Al Yafei, B. Stenger
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: digital rock physics, lattice Boltzmann, carbonate reservoir rock types, Archie exponents, relative permeability, drainage capillary pressure
- Cited by 5 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2010/SCA2010-24.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2010-24)

Digital rock physics results on Abu Dhabi carbonate plugs are compared with laboratory SCAL data, showing generally satisfactory agreement in about three months versus over two years.

The paper validates digital rock physics (DRP) against historical laboratory SCAL data for Lower Cretaceous carbonates from onshore Abu Dhabi. Six core plugs from three rock types (RRT-2, RRT-4, RRT-6) were imaged by X-ray CT at 100 nm to 40 µm, segmented, and simulated with the lattice Boltzmann method for permeability, relative permeability, and drainage capillary pressure, plus electrical and elastic simulations. DRP porosity-permeability, formation factor, velocities, and moduli agree well with lab trends. The cementation exponent m was close to the lab range for RRT2 (1.99 vs 1.96-2.21), but the saturation exponent n for RRT6 was poorly matched (2.74 vs 1.54-1.75). Relative permeability and drainage Pc curves matched steady-state and porous-plate data satisfactorily, while some unsteady-state lab curves were deemed unrealistic. DRP took three months versus over two years for the lab program.

## Computations of Porosity and Permeability of Sparic Carbonate Using Multi-Scale CT Images

- ID: SCA2009-31, year: 2009
- Authors: Abraham S, Andrew B.S. Clark, Abu Dhabi, Taha Al-Dayyani, Amos Nur
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: multi-scale micro-CT, Lattice Boltzmann method, digital rock physics, carbonate permeability, relative permeability, core panoscopy
- Cited by 6 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2009/SCA2009-31.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2009-31)

Multi-scale registered micro-CT (8, 4, 2 micron) and Lattice Boltzmann simulation give porosity, permeability and relative permeability of a heterogeneous carbonate, reasonably matching laboratory data.

Heterogeneous Cretaceous rudist-bearing carbonate plugs make flow properties hard to determine. A 12 mm portion of a 25 mm plug was imaged at 8, 4 and 2 micron voxels, with low-resolution scans guiding physical sub-sampling and selection of the granular (sparic) flow unit. Granular porosity was 0.29 and the vuggy region about 0.19, giving an average of about 0.20 versus 0.21 by MICP and 0.25 from an external lab. Lattice Boltzmann absolute permeability was 1.19 D for the granular volume and 1.2 D for the vuggy volume, versus 0.44 D measured on the larger plug. Two-phase LBM gave Swi 5% and Sor 80% versus 4% and 85% from the simulation model, with Kro in good agreement and Krw matching only up to Sw of 50%. The large vugs are disconnected, so the granular material controls flow.

## Anchoring Methodologies for Pore-Scale Network Models: Application to Relative Permeability and Capillary Pressure Prediction

- ID: SCA2001-15, year: 2001
- Authors: Steven R McDougall, John Cruickshank, Ken S Sorbie
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network model, mercury injection capillary pressure, R-plot, gas-oil relative permeability, non-uniqueness, coordination number
- Cited by 8 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-15.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-15)

Pore network models anchored to MICP data fit 8 samples' R-plots but poorly predict gas-oil relative permeability, because parameter combinations are non-unique.

The paper tests whether pore-scale bond network models can be anchored to mercury injection capillary pressure (MICP) data from 8 reservoir samples to predict gas/oil relative permeability. An analytic two-pore-family model with an accessibility function was fitted to MICP R-plots, matching them well. Direct relative permeability predictions from these anchored parameters were generally poor, even with variable conductivity exponent. The inverse approach, matching relative permeability with a four-parameter model (z, pore size exponent, volume exponent, conductivity exponent in 2.5-4.0), also reproduced the MICP R-plots, aided by a neural network for initial guesses. Analysis shows families of parameter triples give near-identical R-plots but very different relative permeabilities, so MICP anchoring is necessary but not sufficient; one extra independent datum (e.g. NMR T2, intrusion-extrusion loops) is needed.

## Digital Core Laboratory: Rock and Flow Properties Derived from Computer Generated Rocks

- ID: SCA2006-21, year: 2006
- Authors: P.E. Øren, S. Bakke, H.G. Rueslåtten
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: digital rock physics, geologically based reconstruction, pore network modeling, Lattice Boltzmann permeability, Amott wettability index, waterflood relative permeability
- Cited by 6 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-21.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-21)

Geologically reconstructed sandstone models match micro-CT permeability, formation factor and elastic moduli, and network modeling predicts waterflood relative permeabilities, with residual oil rising with initial water saturation.

The paper tests whether process-based reconstructed rocks can predict rock and flow properties of a heterogeneous, poorly consolidated North Sea channel sandstone (porosity 0.23-0.27, 1-10 D). Ten 512^3 realizations at 5.24 µm were built from BSE thin-section input and compared with a micro-CT image using Lattice Boltzmann permeability, finite-difference formation factor and finite-element elastic moduli. The reconstruction overpredicted permeability by 13% (6221 vs 5502 mD) and underpredicted formation factor by 20% (10.9 vs 13.6), with a small systematic underprediction of elastic moduli. Network-model waterflood simulations at Amott index about 0.5 overpredicted centrifuge oil relative permeability when Swi was 0.15, but agreed much better at Swi 0.29, because higher Swi reduces oil film stability and raises Sorw. Predicted relative permeabilities also bracketed steady-state data from two composite cores.

## Pore Shapes and Pore Geometry of Reservoir Rocks from µ-CT Imaging and Digital Image Analysis

- ID: SCA2016-093, year: 2016
- Authors: Matthias Halisch, Mayka Schmitt, Celso Peres Fernandes
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: µ-CT imaging, pore shape classification, Feret caliper, watershed segmentation, sandstone, 3D image analysis
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-093.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-093)

Compares bounding-box, equivalent-diameter and Feret caliper descriptors for 3D pore shape classification in µ-CT images of three sandstones, finding Feret calipers deviate least from true pore geometry.

Pore shape is usually quantified in 2D or by equivalent-sphere measures, which poorly describe irregular 3D pores. The authors imaged Bentheimer, Obernkirchen and Flechtingen sandstones by µ-CT at 1.5 µm voxel size, extracted subvolumes of 250³, 500³ and 1000³ voxels, and separated pore networks into individual pores with a watershed algorithm in Avizo. Pore length, width and thickness were measured with bounding-box (BB), Feret caliper (FC) and equivalent diameter (EqD) methods and used for equancy and shape-class plots. BB and EqD underestimate pore geometry and bias shapes toward cubic or spherical, with mismatches of 10-18% in well-defined rocks like Bentheimer and up to 200% in Flechtingen. FC deviations never exceeded ±20% and gave more realistic plate-like shape classes.

## Pore network simulations coupled with innovative wettability anchoring experiment to predict relative permeability of a mixed-wet rock

- ID: SCA2022-10, year: 2022
- Authors: Mohamed Regaieg, Franck Nono, Titly Farhana Faisal, Clément Varloteaux, Richard Rivenq
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: digital rock physics, pore network modelling, wettability anchoring, ESRGAN super-resolution, mixed-wet small, relative permeability
- Cited by 4 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2022/SCA2022-10.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2022-10)

A micro-CT wettability anchoring experiment constrains pore network simulations on an ESRGAN-enhanced Bentheimer image, and the predicted relative permeability agrees well with an in-house SCAL waterflood.

Predicting relative permeability of mixed-wet rock by digital rock physics is limited by uncertain wettability inputs. The authors developed a fast micro-CT anchoring experiment on a 6 mm Bentheimer sample (spontaneous imbibition, forced imbibition, spontaneous drainage) that gives the oil-wet fraction (53-63%), wettability-radius correlation (mixed-wet small), a spatial correlation length (~500 µm, about 6 pores), Sor and Krw at Sor. A low-resolution image was upscaled 4x with ESRGAN, a 3.6 million element network was extracted by stitching, and 3000 DynaPNM realizations were run, keeping only those matching the anchoring constraints (Pc=0 at Sw 0.4-0.5, Sor 0.18-0.26, Krw(Sor) 0.1-0.2). P10/P50/P90 curves from the blind simulations agreed very well with the envelope of unsteady-state SCAL inversions on a 5 cm by 20 cm Bentheimer plug.

## Multiscale Characterization of Reservoir Rock Microstructure: Combining Small-Angle Neutron Scattering and Image Analysis

- ID: SCA2002-35, year: 2002
- Authors: A.P. Radlinski, M.A. Ioannidis, A. L. Hinde, M. Hainbuchner, M. Baron, H. Rauch, S. R. Kline, Canberra City
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: small-angle neutron scattering, surface fractal, polydisperse spherical pore model, backscatter SEM autocorrelation, mercury intrusion porosimetry, NMR T2 relaxation
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-35.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-35)

Combining SANS/USANS with BSE image analysis gives a pore size distribution from 1 nm to hundreds of microns for a sandstone, consistent with MIP and NMR.

Pore structure of reservoir rock spans nanometers to hundreds of micrometers, and no single technique covers it. For a 450 mD central Australian quartz sandstone (porosity 0.181), SANS, USANS and BSE image autocorrelation were combined to give the absolute scattering cross section over 10 Å to ~1 mm. The pore-rock interface is a surface fractal with D≈2.47–2.5 and an upper cutoff of ~35–60 µm, and a polydisperse spherical pore model puts about 62% of pore volume in the fractal regime. Porosity from the scattering invariant (0.184) and the model fit (0.181) matched core analysis and MIP (0.176). A synthetic mercury capillary pressure curve from the model matched MIP when the entry radius was about four times smaller than the pore radius. A fractal NMR relaxation theory explained the bi-exponential T2 (short fraction 0.41 measured, 0.48 predicted) and a permeability length scale of ~52 µm.

## Quantitative Mineral Analysis by Fourier Transform Infrared Spectroscopy

- ID: SCA1993-08, year: 1993
- Authors: Abigail Matteson, Michael M. Herron
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: FTIR spectroscopy, quantitative mineralogy, nonnegative least squares, clay mineral quantification, KBr pellet, opal-A quartz
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1993/SCA1993-08.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1993-08)

A transmission FTIR pressed-pellet procedure with nonnegative least-squares spectral fitting quantifies 14 minerals in 49 synthetic mixtures to within ±1.2 wt %, without clay size separation.

Quantitative mineralogy from XRD is limited by particle size and orientation effects and by clay fraction separation. The authors developed a transmission FTIR procedure using KBr pressed pellets, with samples ground to about 2.5 µm, microbalance weighing, automated mixing and vacuum pressing. Relative standard deviation of peak absorbance was below ±5%, and auto-mixing cut smectite RSD from 15 to 3%. They tested 49 mixtures of 14 minerals, including extra species of calcite, kaolinite, smectite and chlorite. Whole-spectrum least squares gave an average absolute error of ±2.6 wt %, while nonnegative least squares gave ±1.2 wt % and avoided unphysical negative concentrations such as -14 wt % illite. Quartz and opal-A were separated, and clay types were quantified, but calcite and calc-spar could not be distinguished; summed species agreed better with known values than individual species.

## Resolving Challenges in Analyzing Iron-Rich Samples by X-Ray Diffraction

- ID: SCA2023-047, year: 2023
- Authors: Salah Al-Ofi, Shouxiang Ma
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: X-ray diffraction, Rietveld refinement, siderite, Cu K-alpha fluorescence, mineral library, sample grinding contamination
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2023/SCA2023-047.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2023-047)

Systematic XRD study of iron-rich Chelsea sandstone shows grinding contamination, Cu fluorescence background, and library/fitting-model choice strongly affect mineral identification and quantification.

Iron-rich rocks pose problems for XRD with Cu radiation, so the authors examined sample preparation, instrument, acquisition, phase identification and Rietveld quantification. Samples were Fontainebleau and Indiana limestone outcrops, a multimineral mix, and Chelsea sandstone (shaly, sideritic, 50% mix), ground by mortar, planetary ball mill or mortar grinder and run on Rigaku and PANalytical diffractometers. Ball-mill grinding of Fontainebleau sandstone introduced tungsten carbide contamination growing with time, while Indiana limestone showed none. Siderite fluoresces under Cu radiation, raising background and suppressing other phases. In Indiana limestone, the Refinable Polynomial model improved GoF (11.67 to 7.12) but raised quartz from 1.7% to 9.2%. The AMCSD library gave best GoF but overestimated chlorite (27 vs 8-9 wt%) and underestimated muscovite (17 vs 28 wt%) in the wacke sandstone, so GoF alone is a poor quality indicator.

## Evaluation of the Reliability of Prediction of Petrophysical Data Through Imagery and Pore Network Modelling

- ID: SCA2008-33, year: 2008
- Authors: C. Caubit, G. Hamon, A.P. Sheppard
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: pore network modelling, X-ray micro-tomography, blind test, gas/oil relative permeability, drainage capillary pressure, residual gas saturation
- Cited by 5 papers in the corpus, cites 6
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-33.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-33)

A blind test of ANU X-ray micro-CT and Numerical Rocks thin-section pore network modelling against Total lab data finds reliable porosity and Pc only when microporosity and clay are captured.

The paper evaluates two pore-network prediction workflows, ANU (X-ray micro-CT) and Numerical Rocks (thin-section, geologically based reconstruction), in a blind test against Total's in-house measurements. Samples were outcrop/artificial (Berea, Clashach, Fontainebleau, Aerolith 10) and reservoir rocks. Porosity, permeability, mercury drainage Pc, gas/oil relative permeability and residual gas saturation were predicted using Valvatne, Piri and eCore simulators. Porosity was underestimated by about 1.5 PU for clean rocks, with the Berea error of 5.7 PU reduced to 0.5 PU by adding clay-derived microporosity. Permeability errors averaged about ±40%, with a factor of 5 scatter between NR realisations. Pc and oil relative permeability were reproduced when porosity, clay and Pc were well captured, but krg was poor, Sgrw required unrealistic contact angles, and the approach is not yet an industrial tool.

## A Framework to Validate Digital Rock Technology

- ID: SCA2011-28, year: 2011
- Authors: J. Schembre-McCabe, R. Salazar-Tio, G. Ball, J. Kamath, San Ramon
- Method: review or workflow
- Keywords: digital rock validation, experimental design, micro-CT segmentation, representative elementary volume, uncertainty analysis, pore network modeling
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-28.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-28)

A framework compares digital rock computed property ranges, from experimental design on segmentation uncertainty, with laboratory uncertainty ranges; a sandstone example shows overlap for most properties except elastic ones.

The paper proposes a workflow to validate digital rock computations against lab data while accounting for uncertainty on both sides. Steps are micro-CT imaging with REV assessment via two-point correlation and lineal path functions, three-phase segmentation, simulation with full factorial experimental design, and range comparison. Computed ranges (mean ± mσ) are judged useful if they fall within the experimental range or a user-specified error band. In the demonstration, a 5 mm mini-plug imaged at 2.6 µm/pixel had a correlation length of about 100 µm, and 3 segmentations × 3 gray-phase porosities were run on a 1100³ volume. Porosity, permeability and formation factor computed ranges overlapped the experimental uncertainty, with wider computed bands, while elastic properties, run on five sub-volumes below REV, were mostly not within the experimental range. ANOVA showed permeability depends only on segmentation, while porosity and Swi depend on both segmentation and microporosity.

## Investigation into the Reliability of Predictive Pore-Scale Modeling for Siliciclastic Reservoir Rocks

- ID: SCA2010-23, year: 2010
- Authors: Olivier Lopez, Alexander Mock, Jostein Skretting, Egil Boye Petersen Jr, Pål-Eric Øren, Alf Birger Rustad
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore-scale modeling, pore network model, Lattice-Boltzmann permeability, relative permeability, process-based rock reconstruction, Amott wettability index
- Cited by 7 papers in the corpus, cites 3
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2010/SCA2010-23.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2010-23)

Process-based pore-scale modeling of 28 Norwegian siliciclastic reservoir samples predicts porosity, permeability and oil-water relative permeability in good agreement with core data and field trends.

The study tests whether predictive pore-scale modeling is reliable across many siliciclastic reservoir rocks. Twenty-eight samples from six NCS fields (porosity 0.14–0.43, permeability 5 mD–20 D, clay up to 0.19) were reconstructed as 3D models from BSEM thin sections using process-based modeling, three realizations each. Absolute permeability came from Lattice-Boltzmann simulation, and primary drainage and waterflood relative permeabilities from pore-network oil/water simulations with wettability set from SCAL data. Predicted porosity, permeability, Swi, Sorw, krw(Sorw) and relative permeability curves fell within experimental ranges and reproduced field trends. Simulated Amott indices ranged from about -0.31 to 0.65 depending on field. The work was completed within three months, and the authors conclude that the approach is reliable for weakly water-wet to weakly oil-wet conditions.

## Pore-Scale Modelling: Effects of Network Properties on Predictive Capabilities

- ID: SCA2012-35, year: 2012
- Authors: Nasiru Idowu, Cyril Nardi, Haili Long, Pål-Eric Øren, Numerical Rocks
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network extraction, skeletonization, micro-CT, relative permeability, thinning algorithm, grain-based algorithm
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-35.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-35)

Three skeletonization methods applied to micro-CT images of sandstones give different pore networks but similar multiphase flow predictions, which agree with grid simulations and experiments when topology is preserved.

Pore network extraction is non-unique, so the authors compared three skeletonization algorithms: two pore-based thinning methods (ImageJ/Fiji and Avizo DOHT) and a grain-based algorithm. They applied them to micro-CT images of Berea (two images), Bentheimer and Fontainebleau sandstones and built networks with identical geometric characterization. Pore counts differed by 7% to 128%, with the grain-based networks having fewer pores, higher coordination numbers and fewer isolated and dead-end pores. Grain-based networks gave absolute permeabilities closest to LBM grid values (e.g. 1,213 vs 1,345 mD for Berea_01), while DOHT networks gave the lowest (666 mD) and highest formation factors. Drainage capillary pressure, relative permeability, Swi, imbibition curves (water-, mixed- and oil-wet) and Sorw were similar across networks, and agreed with grid-predicted and steady-state experimental data for Berea and Bentheimer.

## Computation of Relative Permeability Functions in 3D Digital Rocks by a Fractional Flow Approach Using the Lattice Boltzmann Method

- ID: SCA2012-36, year: 2012
- Authors: Giuseppe De Prisco, Jonas Toelke, Moustafa R Dernaika, Abu Dhabi
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: lattice Boltzmann method, digital rock physics, relative permeability, fractional flow boundary conditions, representative elementary volume, mixed wettability
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 3
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-36.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-36)

A GPU-based lattice Boltzmann method with PID-controlled inlet boundary conditions simulates fractional-flow drainage and imbibition relative permeability in a digital carbonate, matching experimental imbibition curves.

Numerical relative permeability in digital rocks is hindered by unknown inlet/outlet conditions and by uncertainty over whether a subsample is Darcian and representative. The authors present a multiphase lattice Boltzmann scheme with annular wetting/non-wetting inlet zones and PID feedback control that sets target fractional flows, plus a moment-based screening of subsamples to select a Darcian REV. They simulated primary drainage and imbibition on a 500³ voxel (65 nm) image of a Middle Eastern carbonate (0.82 mD measured, 1.3 mD digital; Swi 0.124) using about 28 GPUs and roughly 30M iterations per cycle. Drainage used a 30° contact angle and imbibition used either a uniform 135° or a mixed-wet assignment based on digital MICP. Simulated curves matched measured steady-state imbibition endpoints and crossover well. The uniform-angle case deviated at low water saturation, and the mixed-wet case gave very low water permeability there.

## Statistical Synthesis of Image Analysis and Mercury Porosimetry for Multiscale Pore Structure Characterization

- ID: SCA2003-10, year: 2003
- Authors: E. Amirtharaj, M.A. Ioannidis, I.F. Macdonald
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: mercury intrusion porosimetry, surface fractal dimension, two-point correlation function, pore size distribution, pore-to-throat aspect ratio, BSEM image analysis
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-10.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-10)

Combines BSEM image-derived two-point correlation with MIP fractal scaling to build pore size distributions over 0.01-1000 μm, revealing pore-to-throat aspect ratios in sandstones and carbonates.

Pore structure in reservoir rock spans many orders of magnitude, and no single technique covers it. The authors fuse backscatter SEM image analysis (S2(r), above about 1 μm) with mercury intrusion porosimetry, using the surface fractal scaling I(Q) ∝ Q^(D-6) to extrapolate scattering intensity to small sizes. They verify that fractal dimensions from MIP and imaging agree reasonably for Berea sandstone, vuggy Silurian dolomites and silty Chatsworth sandstones (D about 2.3-2.95). Inverting the extended I(Q) with a spherical pore model gives a pore size distribution over five orders of magnitude, which is compared with MIP curves. Berea matches MIP with a pore-to-throat aspect ratio of about 3, poorly sorted clayey sandstones need ratios up to about 30, and carbonates show bimodal distributions with ratios of 1-20 that no single constant ratio fits. Permeability is also predicted within a factor of two from porosity and the correlation length Λ.

## Drainage and Imbibition Capillary Pressure Curves of Carbonate Reservoir Rocks by Digital Rock Physics

- ID: SCA2012-56, year: 2012
- Authors: Y. Mu, Q. Fang, C. Baldwin, J. Töelke, A. Grader, M. Dernaika, Z. Kalam, Abu Dhabi
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: digital rock physics, lattice Boltzmann, capillary pressure, drainage and imbibition, GPU, carbonate
- Cited by 4 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-56.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-56)

Lattice Boltzmann simulations on a micro-CT image of a micritic carbonate predict drainage and imbibition Pc curves that reasonably match MICP and centrifuge data.

The paper presents a digital rock physics workflow to compute primary drainage and imbibition capillary pressure curves in carbonates, simulating porous plate experiments. A 3D D3Q19 immiscible lattice Boltzmann model (Rothman-Keller type, two relaxation times) is accelerated on GPUs. It was applied to a 500^3 voxel image (64 nm resolution) of a tight micritic carbonate (sample #33, RRT#6; lab porosity 24.3%, 0.82 mD; DRP 23.7%, 1.3 mD). DRP-derived NMR pore size and MICP throat size distributions captured the pore structure, though small throats were limited by resolution. Drainage Pc matched MICP and centrifuge data reasonably, with Swr about 14% from DRP versus about 10% from centrifuge. Imbibition showed DRP crossing Pc=0 at lower Sw, suggesting more oil-wet behavior from the assumed contact angles, while Sor agreed closely with the lab.

## Pore Structure and Hydrodynamics in Sandstones

- ID: SCA1996-07, year: 1996
- Authors: G. Chauveteau, L. Nabzar, Y. El Attar, C. Jacquin
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: grain and pore throat model, permeability-porosity relation, Fontainebleau sandstone, polymer depletion, mercury injection, quartz overgrowth
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1996/SCA1996-07.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1996-07)

A grain and pore throat model links permeability to grain size, porosity and hydrodynamic throat diameter; mercury injection overestimates throat size by 1.7–1.9 times in sharp-edged media.

The paper proposes a grain and pore throat (GPT) model in which viscous dissipation is localized in pore throats and grains set the throat spacing. It predicts k ∝ dg² for unconsolidated packs and k = c·dgo²[(Φ-Φ1)/(Φo-Φ1)]⁴ for sandstones cemented by uniform overgrowth. Glass bead and silicon carbide packs both show quadratic permeability–grain size dependence, with SiC constant about 2.43 times smaller. For Fontainebleau sandstones, a residual porosity Φ1 of 2% gives a linear log-log fit with slope 0.25 down to low permeabilities, unlike the plain porosity power law. Hydrodynamic diameters from polymer depletion and latex deposition compared with mercury injection diameters give dHg ≈ 1.7 dh for SiC packs, 1.2 dh for bead packs and 1.9 dh for sandstones. Mercury injection therefore overestimates the minimum restriction diameter dmin by a factor of about 3 to 4.

## Quantitative X-Ray CT for SCAL Plug Homogeneity Assessment

- ID: SCA2013-004, year: 2013
- Authors: Jos G. Maas, Albert Hebing
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: X-ray CT, Hounsfield units, mini-permeameter, permeability variation, plug homogeneity, beam hardening
- Cited by 6 papers in the corpus, cites 4
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2013/SCA2013-004.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2013-004)

A theoretical model links CT Hounsfield standard deviation to permeability variation in core plugs, validated against mini-permeameter data on 95 plugs, giving objective homogeneity screening limits.

Visual grey-scale CT inspection of SCAL plugs is subjective, so the authors derive a model relating the standard deviation of Hounsfield values to the coefficient of variation of permeability, V ≈ (d/a)σHU, using a linear HU–porosity relation and an exponential poro-perm relation. It was tested on 95 plugs (sandstone, chalk, outcrop, sintered glass) using two longitudinal CT tomograms per plug and about 80 mini-permeameter readings per plug. For 62 plugs passing a cutoff of σφ/φ < 0.35, the predicted versus measured V correlation had a Pearson coefficient of 0.56 with a slope near unity, significant at over 99%. Deviations arose from local edge heterogeneity, beam-hardening cupping, and high-absorption minerals such as pyrite, and the model failed for vertically drilled layered plugs. Table 1 gives maximum allowable σHU for chosen V, e.g. 25 HU for V=0.1 in a sandstone set.

## Distribution of Clays in Sandstones by Image Analysis: Quantification, Representativity and Effects on Petrophysical Properties

- ID: SCA1994-14, year: 1994
- Authors: Claudine Durand
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: SEM image analysis, clay distribution, variogram representativity, covariance function, Kozeny-Carman permeability, sandstone
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1994/SCA1994-14.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1994-14)

SEM backscattered image analysis quantifies clay distribution in sandstones, with variograms checking representativity; permeability formulas fit model sandstones but fail for North Sea reservoir sandstones.

The study quantifies clay distribution in Fontainebleau, model clayey and North Sea reservoir sandstones using backscattered SEM images and image analysis. Variograms of mineral amounts across adjacent images determine how many images are needed for representative means, from a few images in homogeneous samples to about twenty, or more than the sample allowed, in reservoir samples. Granulometric parameters (perimeter, maximal projection) and geostatistical covariance parameters (volumic surface area, range) were derived, and image-based mineral contents agreed fairly well with elemental analysis and plug porosity. Poiseuille, Kozeny-Carman and Jacquin relationships generally overpredicted permeability. They could be adjusted for model sandstones, but failed for reservoir sandstones, where diagenetic clays change pore geometry in ways the parameters do not capture. Image processing such as erosion and dilation strongly changed the measured porosity and surface area, so it was avoided.

## Case Study in Validating Capillary Pressure Curves, Relative Permeability and Resistivity Index of Carbonates from X-Ray Micro Tomography Images

- ID: SCA2010-02, year: 2010
- Authors: Zubair Kalam, Taha Al Dayyani, Andrew Clark, Sven Roth, Cyril Nardi, Olivier Lopez, Pål-Eric Øren
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: X-ray micro tomography, pore network modeling, carbonate relative permeability, formation resistivity factor, saturation exponent, capillary pressure
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2010/SCA2010-02.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2010-02)

Micro-CT images and pore-network modeling of three Middle East carbonate plugs predict permeability, resistivity and two-phase properties; they match lab data well except where unresolved microporosity matters.

The study tests whether micro-CT imaging with pore-network modeling (e-Core) can replace conventional SCAL on carbonates. Three plugs from a Lower Cretaceous Middle East reservoir were imaged at ESRF (voxel 7.39 µm for grainstone Sn-5 at 5663 mD; 0.28 µm for Sn-34 at 2.92 mD and Sn-40 at 0.02 mD). Permeability and formation factor were computed on the grid models, and capillary pressure, relative permeability and resistivity index were simulated with a quasi-static network model. For Sn-5, permeability matched (5114 vs 5336 mD) but porosity was 19% vs 26.8% and Swi was too low because of unresolved microporosity. For Sn-34, the m exponent (2.22 vs 2.32) and end points matched closely. For Sn-40, permeability (0.42 vs 0.02 mD) and formation factor (172 vs 67) differed, and no lab relative permeability could be measured. The authors conclude the method is a fast alternative that also allows wettability sensitivity studies.

## Combining Electrical Measurements and Mercury Porosimetry to Predict Permeability

- ID: SCA2012-28, year: 2012
- Authors: Douglas Ruth, Dean Emeritus, Craig Lindsay, Core Specialist, Mark Allen, Lead Reservoir Engineer West Africa
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: mercury injection porosimetry, Purcell permeability model, formation factor, Archie equation, tortuosity, pore throat size distribution
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-28.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-28)

Extends Purcell's equation using an REV model, combining mercury porosimetry and formation factor to predict permeability within a factor of two for 24 Ghana samples over seven decades.

The paper revisits Purcell's capillary-pressure-based permeability prediction, deriving it from a representative elemental volume (single tortuous tube) effective-medium model in which tortuosity is obtained from formation factor (F = tau^2/phi). It was applied to 24 Turonian samples from offshore Ghana with mercury injection curves, corrected for surface pore invasion, plus Archie parameters fitted to 35 companion formation factor measurements. Predictions without fitting parameters cover almost seven decades of permeability, with only one point outside a factor of two, mean under-prediction of about 13% and absolute mean error of about 34%. Using default Archie values (a=1, m=2) raised absolute error to about 40%. The success is explained by the permeability integral being dominated by the largest pore throats, so microporosity has no influence; applicability to carbonates, fines-bearing rocks and overburden effects remains open. A test protocol and extension to drill cuttings and digital rock are proposed.

## 3D Imaging and Flow Characterization of the Pore Space of Carbonate Core Samples

- ID: SCA2006-23, year: 2006
- Authors: Mark Knackstedt, Christoph Arns, Abid Ghous, Arthur Sakellariou, Tim Senden, Adrian Sheppard, Rob Sok, Holger Averdunk, W. Val Pinczewski, Girija S. Padhy, Marios A. Ioannidis
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: micro-CT, digital rock physics, carbonate microporosity, pore network extraction, lattice Boltzmann permeability, MICP simulation
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 3
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-23.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-23)

Micro-CT images of model, outcrop and reservoir carbonates give MICP, permeability and pore networks matching experiments, and a hybrid scheme accounts for microporosity in flow.

Carbonate pore systems span many scales, and macropore connectivity and microporosity control flow. Four carbonates were imaged by micro-CT at 2.6-11 µm voxels: two artificial vuggy samples (SC4, SC16), Mt. Gambier limestone, and a West Texas reservoir carbonate. Image-based drainage simulations matched experimental MICP curves, and lattice-Boltzmann permeability agreed reasonably with measurements for the resolved samples. Pore networks extracted from the images differed strongly from Castlegate sandstone, with volume-weighted coordination numbers above 30 and higher pore-to-throat aspect ratios. In the West Texas sample about 3% porosity was resolved against about 20% total from wet-dry imaging, and a Stokes-Brinkman hybrid scheme with tracer random-walk showed microporosity yields slower, more dispersed flow when macropores are poorly connected.

## A Comparative Study of Three Pore-Scale Reconstruction and Pore-Network Extraction Techniques

- ID: SCA2008-36, year: 2008
- Authors: Arjen Cense, Fons Marcelis
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: pore-network modeling, micro-CT reconstruction, process-based reconstruction, pore architecture models, Lattice-Boltzmann permeability, primary drainage capillary pressure
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-36.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-36)

Three third-party pore-scale reconstruction and network extraction methods (micro-CT, PAMs, process-based) are compared on a clay-rich sandstone; single-phase properties are predicted reasonably, primary drainage multiphase properties only partly.

Pore-network modeling could shorten special core analysis, but reconstruction methods are usually tested on clean sandstones. Here micro-CT, pore architecture models (PAMs) and process-based reconstruction were applied to a clay-rich sandstone (porosity 0.242, permeability about 140-160 mD, about 10% clay). Voxel models were compared via Lattice-Boltzmann permeability and two-point correlation against BSEM images and lab data. Porosity matched within 10%, and permeability matched for two of three methods (micro-CT overestimated by a factor of 3). Networks were simulated for primary drainage: relative permeability agreed with centrifuge data for all three, but capillary pressure curve shape was poorly reproduced, and connate water saturation was matched only when clay was modeled explicitly (process-based Swc 0.17 versus experiment 0.17; PAMs about zero). The authors caution that conclusions apply only to this rock.

## Characterisation of Carbonate Reservoir Heterogeneity Using Probe Permeability Images, Petrography and Borehole Image Log Data

- ID: SCA2001-41, year: 2001
- Authors: Graham F. Aplin, Ajay K. Sapru
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: probe permeametry, borehole image logs, nodular limestone, image thresholding, permeability heterogeneity, karstification
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-41.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-41)

Probe permeability maps, petrography and microresistivity image logs are combined to characterise permeability heterogeneity in a nodular limestone reservoir, using image thresholding to extend permeability classes beyond cored intervals.

Carbonate reservoirs are heterogeneous from the reservoir to the micro-scale, and this causes large permeability variations. A nodular limestone reservoir with a complex diagenetic history was studied: early compaction and porosity loss, then late-burial porosity generation linked to thermal karstification. Probe permeability was measured on a 4 mm grid on slabbed core surfaces to build permeability images, and large-format thin sections were prepared from the same sites. Petrography links the permeability variations to microporosity and vuggy porosity in corroded internodule matrix, cementation within nodules, and very high permeability in vugs, moulds and fractures. Microresistivity image logs were divided into image facies based on the nodule to internodule matrix proportion and matrix mineralogy. Core calibration shows each facies spans a wide permeability range. Conductivity cut-offs calibrated to probe permeability define four classes: tight (<0.5 mD), low (0.5-10 mD), moderate (10-100 mD) and high (>100 mD). Thresholding gives a continuous trace of each class's abundance and its cumulative abundance, which identifies the distribution of high-permeability zones.

## Two-Phase Flow Rock-Typing : Another Approach

- ID: SCA2003-13, year: 2003
- Authors: Gerald Hamon, Miguel Bennes
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: relative permeability, rock-typing, wettability index, waterflood residual oil saturation, height above contact, coreflood sampling
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-13.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-13)

Using Fontainebleau sandstone and two field cases, the paper shows pore-geometry rock-types fail for waterflood Kr when wettability varies; multivariate, process-dependent rock-types are needed.

Routine rock-types built from porosity, permeability, capillary pressure and facies are often assumed to also define relative permeability, whatever the recovery process. The authors test this on Fontainebleau sandstone (water-wet and aged) and on two field datasets: a West Africa sandstone (13 gas floods, 16 waterfloods) and a carbonate (13 waterfloods). In strongly water-wet Fontainebleau and in the gas-oil floods of Field A, residual saturation and oil Kr exponent correlate well with porosity, permeability or Pc shape factor. For Field A waterfloods, Sorw ranged 0.1–0.225 and Krw(Sorw) 0.09–0.45, with no single pore-geometry indicator explaining them; Sorw and Krw(Sorw) trended with height above the water-oil contact, indicating wettability control. In the carbonate, Swi correlates with permeability but Sorw does not, so rock-types for oil in place differ from those for reserves. Combining pore-geometry and wettability indicators captured most Kr variability, and rock-types should depend on the recovery process.

## Pore Network Models to Calculate Transport and Electrical Properties of Single or Dual-Porosity Rocks

- ID: SCA2005-35, year: 2005
- Authors: S. Békri, O. Vizika
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network model, resistivity index, saturation exponent, non-Archie behavior, mercury porosimetry, gas/oil relative permeability
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-35.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-35)

A 3-D pore network model built from mercury porosimetry predicts Fontainebleau gas/oil relative permeability and resistivity index, showing pore-body/throat correlation and wettability control saturation exponent and non-Archie behavior.

Pore structure controls transport and electrical properties, but different network parameters can match the same capillary pressure curve. A 3-D cubic pore network with converging-diverging throats and angular cross-sections was built from mercury porosimetry for a Fontainebleau sandstone (22.5% porosity, 1963 mD). Five scenarios varying the pore-volume/throat-radius exponent λp and throat-length exponent λt were tested. Uncorrelated pore volumes (λp=0) matched Pc but overestimated gas relative permeability, whereas λp=1–2 with a fraction of small throats matched Kr; run 5 (λp=2, coordination ≈4, FF=17) agreed with microtomography (Z=3.75). Saturation exponents were 1.91–1.94 for correlated cases versus 2.82 for uncorrelated ones, and oil-wet networks showed early upward-bending resistivity index while water-wet ones bent only below 3% Sw. Preliminary dual-porosity simulations on a carbonate (33.8%, 115 mD) gave roughly Archie behavior with n above 2.

## Validation of Fundamental Carbonate Reservoir Core Properties Using Digital Rock Physics

- ID: SCA2012-19, year: 2012
- Authors: Olivier Lopez, Alex Mock, Pål Eric Øren, Haili Long, Zubair Kalam, Volker Vahrenkamp, Muhamed Gibrata, Samy Seraj, Soman Chacko, Mohamed Al Hammadi, Habeeba Al Hosni, Hani Sahn, Antonio Vizamora
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: digital rock physics, micro-CT upscaling, carbonate rock types, formation resistivity factor, primary drainage capillary pressure, NMR T2 relaxation
- Cited by 4 papers in the corpus, cites 9
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-19.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-19)

Multi-scale micro-CT imaging and upscaled digital rock modeling of 100+ Middle-Eastern carbonate plugs reproduces measured porosity, permeability, FRF, m, Pc and NMR T2 well.

Carbonate rocks are multi-scale, so digital rock predictions from small homogeneous samples need upscaling to plug and whole-core scale. Over 100 1.5-inch plugs from four giant Middle-Eastern carbonate fields were scanned by micro-CT (12.7 and 19 µm), with micro plugs imaged at 0.28–5 µm and micro-porosity models built from BSEM. Properties were computed at pore scale (Lattice-Boltzmann permeability, random-walk FRF and NMR, network Pc) and upscaled with steady-state methods. Against lab data (porosity 0.11–0.35, permeability 0.63–2983 mD), only about 4% of samples fell outside the ±2 porosity unit range and about 7% outside the factor-2 permeability range, mostly below 10 mD. FRF, m, drainage Pc, Leverett J-functions and NMR T2 also matched well, with differences attributed to smaller, less representative MICP samples.

## Can Network Modelling Predict Two-Phase Flow Functions?

- ID: SCA2011-29, year: 2011
- Authors: K.S. Sorbie, A. Skauge
- Method: review or workflow
- Keywords: pore network modelling, a priori prediction, mixed wettability, relative permeability, digital rock, network extraction
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-29.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-29)

Argues that a priori network-model prediction of kr, Pc and Sor in mixed-wet rocks needs ~15-26 parameters, more than the ~5-8 the data contain, so it is not true prediction.

The paper critically examines whether pore-network modelling can predict two-phase relative permeability, capillary pressure and residual oil a priori. It splits the workflow into four stages: digital rock construction, network extraction, wettability assignment and pore-scale physics. The authors estimate parameter counts of 2-4, 4-6, 7-12 and 2-4 respectively, totalling about 15-26, against only ~5-8 parameters of information in a typical kr/Pc/Sor dataset. Wettability assignment is judged the most parameterised and least validated stage. Literature examples support this: the Berea drainage kr match uses Swi set by assigned clay content, and the blind tests of Caubit et al. failed to predict Sgrw. Network models remain useful for single-phase properties, explanation of trends, interpolation/extrapolation of anchored data, three-phase properties, mineralogy effects and residual oil structure.

## Density Functional Hydrodynamics in Multiscale Pore Systems: Chemical Potential Drive

- ID: SCA2019-002, year: 2019
- Authors: Oleg Dinariev, Nikolay Evseev, Denis Klemin
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: density functional hydrodynamics, chemical potential drive, digital rock upscaling, subresolution porosity, steady-state relative permeability, carbonate
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2019/SCA2019-002.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2019-002)

Upscales density functional hydrodynamics to a chemical potential drive model for unresolved pores, coupled with DFH in resolved pores, and validates it on a carbonate micro-CT model.

Multiscale carbonate pores make it impossible to resolve both small pores and large vugs in one micro-CT image. The authors derive a Chemical Potential Drive (CPD) transport model from the DFH equations, which reduces to Darcy's law for single-phase flow and generalizes it with cross-terms and diffusion. They couple DFH in resolved pores with CPD in the subresolution matrix (DFH+CPD) in the DHD simulator. On a 26.2% porosity, 19.4 mD grainstone, a 0.82 µm high-resolution model and a 2.46 µm low-resolution model of the same region gave absolute permeabilities of 17.3 and 18.2 mD, with comparable steady-state relative permeability curves. The workflow was then applied to a 4.92×4.92×2.46 mm model covering nearly the whole 8 mm mini plug, giving 19.8 mD.

## Prediction of relative permeability and fast wettability assessment using Digital Rock Physics: An operational study on a Reservoir Sandstone

- ID: SCA2023-026, year: 2023
- Authors: Mohamed Regaieg, Titly Farhana Faisal, Franck Nono, Fabrice Pairoys, Victor Fernandes, Cyril Caubit
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: digital rock physics, ESRGAN super-resolution, pore network modelling, wettability anchoring experiment, relative permeability, micro-CT
- Cited by 4 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2023/SCA2023-026.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2023-026)

A blind-test DRP workflow (ESRGAN, pore network modelling, wettability anchoring) predicted waterflood relative permeability of a reservoir sandstone, agreeing with SCAL on the tested facies and a second facies.

The study tests an operational digital rock physics workflow for predicting relative permeability on a weakly oil-wet reservoir sandstone, as a blind test before SCAL. Low-resolution micro-CT images (5.36 µm) were enhanced 4x with ESRGAN, which reduced segmentation uncertainty in porosity, permeability and drainage capillary pressure relative to low-resolution images. Pore networks of about 8 million elements were extracted from 5000³ voxel images, and a fast micro-CT wettability anchoring experiment (spontaneous imbibition and drainage, end-point measurements) constrained the simulations. Hundreds of pore network realizations were filtered against the anchoring Sor and Krw and ranked into P10, P50 and P90 curves. After breakthrough and before ROS, the curves agreed well with unsteady-state SCAL (328 mD, 22% porosity) in relative permeability, fractional flow, Kr ratio and Buckley-Leverett recovery. Applying the same wettability to a second facies (299 mD, 23.3%) without a new anchoring test also gave good agreement, with turnaround under 2 weeks, about 12 times faster than SCAL.

## Pore-Level Validation of Representative Pore Networks Obtained from Micro-CT Images

- ID: SCA2007_15, year: 2007
- Authors: J. Y. Arns, A. P. Sheppard, C. H. Arns, M. A. Knackstedt, A. Yelkhovsky, W. V. Pinczewski
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: pore network extraction, micro-CT, lattice Boltzmann permeability, throat volume factor, wetting phase connectivity, covering radius transform
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_15.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_15)

Pore networks extracted from micro-CT images reproduce lattice-Boltzmann permeability for large volumes (>10,000 pores), while image-based analysis shows corner wetting phase disconnects as saturation falls.

The paper tests pore network models extracted from micro-CT images of Castlegate sandstone, an unconsolidated sand pack and Mt. Gambier limestone against direct voxel-based lattice-Boltzmann permeability. Networks are built by watershed pore partitioning of the EDT, with pore merging and a throat volume factor as free parameters. Network permeability overestimates image permeability at small volumes (about 30% for Castlegate at 200^3, 50% for sand, 100% for limestone) but agrees within about 2-10% at large volumes. A throat volume factor of about 0.4 gives the best match, while pore merging has little effect on permeability. Image-based covering radius transform analysis shows corner wetting phase becomes increasingly disconnected with decreasing saturation (Castlegate below Sw about 0.4, sand pack about 0.2), which could strongly affect resistivity index.

## Relative permeability assessment in a giant carbonate reservoir using Digital Rock Physics

- ID: SCA2012-03, year: 2012
- Authors: Zubair Kalam, Samy Seraj, Zahid Bhatti, Alex Mock, Pål Eric Øren, Vegar Ravlo, Olivier Lopez
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: digital rock physics, relative permeability, carbonate reservoir, pore network modeling, steady-state upscaling, micro-CT
- Cited by 4 papers in the corpus, cites 5
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-03.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-03)

Blind study comparing digital rock physics water-oil imbibition relative permeability with steady-state lab tests on carbonate rock types, finding good agreement when oil-wet wettability is assumed.

The paper validates digital rock physics (DRP) predictions of water-oil imbibition relative permeability against steady-state composite-core tests on carbonate plugs from a giant Middle East reservoir. The study was blind: the DRP contractor received only fluid properties and wettability preference (strongly oil-wet, from Amott-Harvey tests). Plugs were imaged by micro-CT (19 µm, with microplugs at 0.28–5 µm and BSEM for microporosity), pore-network and Lattice-Boltzmann simulations were run per pore class, and results were upscaled by steady-state methods. Six rock types (RRT 6, 7, 8a, 8b, 11, 15; porosity 13–33%, permeability 10 mD to several Darcies) were compared. DRP porosity, permeability, Swi and endpoints were generally close to lab values, and relative permeability curves matched well, although RRT 11 and 15 showed larger plug-to-plug spread. The authors conclude DRP gives fast, reliable SCAL data for heterogeneous oil-wet carbonates.

## Improving Digital Rock Physics Predictive Potential for Relative Permeabilities from Equivalent Pore Networks

- ID: SCA2013-017, year: 2013
- Authors: Nasiru Idowu, Cyril Nardi, Haili Long, Pål-Eric Øren, Igor Bondino
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network extraction, relative permeability, throat volume allocation, digital rock physics, Clashach sandstone, mercury injection capillary pressure
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2013/SCA2013-017.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2013-017)

Pore network element lengths explain permeability differences between extracted networks, while pore volume allocated to throats explains counter-intuitive relative permeability trends; grid-computed Pc curves can constrain this allocation.

Pore networks extracted from digital images of Clashach sandstone by different methods gave differing absolute and relative permeabilities, and some showed counter-intuitive water-wet versus oil-wet trends. Swapping pore and throat length distributions between topologically identical networks (S2_N2 and S2_N3) made permeabilities converge (about 2,305 vs 2,314 mD) and relative permeability curves coincide, showing that element lengths cause the differences. Increasing the fraction of pore volume allocated to throats from 15% to 50% (and 9% to 60% for a sand pack network) restored the expected wettability trends. Increasing throat volume shifted relative permeability curves and crossover to higher Sw. Comparing mercury-injection Pc curves simulated on the image grid with network results identified the throat volume fraction (37% for a 1,510 mD sandstone) that best matched, without experimental Pc data.

## Rapid Mineral Analysis by Fourier Transform Infrared Spectroscopy

- ID: SCA1988-09, year: 1988
- Authors: Hamish Adam, Don Harville, Daniel Meer, David Freeman
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: FTIR spectroscopy, quantitative mineralogy, Beer's law, KBr pellet, clay minerals, log calibration
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1988/SCA1988-09.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1988-09)

Describes the Mineralog FTIR method for rapid quantitative rock mineralogy, tested on synthetic mixtures to about 5 wt% accuracy, and its uses in log calibration and formation damage screening.

Conventional mineral analysis by XRD, thin section and chemical methods is slow and costly, so mineral data are often unavailable for completion decisions. The authors describe the Mineralog FTIR technique, using KBr pellets of samples ground to under 2 µm, a Perkin-Elmer 1720 spectrometer (16 scans in about 3 minutes, 2 cm-1 resolution), and Beer's law with statistical and peak-fitting comparison against pure-mineral calibration spectra. More than 300 synthetic mixtures, from binary to 12 minerals, were analyzed; minerals were measured to better than 5 wt% at 95% confidence, with r² of 0.97-0.99 for quartz, calcite, anhydrite and total clay. Limitations include inability to separate illite from smectite or muscovite, and difficulty with minor nonstoichiometric carbonates. Applications discussed are log calibration (clay volume, resistivity, matrix density index), formation damage screening and core sampling; samples as small as 0.5 g can be analyzed.

## Dual-Range FT-IR Mineralogy and the Analysis of Sedimentary Formations

- ID: SCA1997-29, year: 1997
- Authors: Michael M. Herron, Abigail Matteson, Gale Gustavson
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: FT-IR mineralogy, far-IR spectroscopy, KBr pellet, mineral standards, kaolinite speciation, geochemical log lithology
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1997/SCA1997-29.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1997-29)

Combining mid- and far-IR spectra with an expanded standards set improves quantitative FT-IR mineralogy of oilfield samples, validated by reconstructing measured elemental chemistry to about ±1 wt%.

Quantitative FT-IR mineralogy was extended to handle natural variability in oilfield samples after more than 1000 analyses. The new procedure merges mid-IR and far-IR spectra (300-5200 cm-1), matches KBr blank scattering, optimizes spectral offset, applies wavelet-based weighting, and uses end-member feldspar standards. The standards set grew from 14 to 29 minerals (49 species). Re-analysis of the earlier artificial-mixture test set showed that adding far-IR and the expanded standards reduced errors for most minerals (e.g. illite 2.6 to 1.6 wt%, calcite 1.8 to 1.0 wt%), with accuracy about ±1 wt% for most. For 45 samples from a European well, elemental concentrations reconstructed from mineralogy matched measured Si, Ca, K and Mg well, while Ti, Th, Gd and U were underestimated owing to organic matter and heavy minerals. Observations included kaolinite order speciation (detrital versus authigenic), illite varieties, and muscovite averaging 19% of illite, which XRD would likely miss.

## The Uses of Surface Area Data Obtained on Reservoir Core Samples

- ID: SCA1998-39, year: 1998
- Authors: Robert D East
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: specific surface area, irreducible water saturation, cation exchange capacity, Qv excess conductivity, cementation exponent, Kozeny-Carman
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1998/SCA1998-39.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1998-39)

Surface area measured on Australasian reservoir cores correlates with permeability, irreducible water, CEC/Qv and cementation exponent, supporting its routine measurement for petrophysical interpretation.

Surface area is rarely measured in routine core analysis, although it influences permeability, irreducible water saturation and excess conductivity. Over a two-year project, polar molecule adsorption surface area was measured on cores from Australasian reservoirs (Cooper, Bonaparte, Malay basins) and compared with other petrophysical data. Surface area correlates with permeability and 50 psi irreducible water saturation within a reservoir, though no universal permeability correlation exists. A 15 nm water film assumption roughly predicted irreducible saturation. Surface area is linearly related to Qv, giving surface charge densities of 0.30 and 0.34 C/m2, and it can serve as a shaliness indicator and Archie/non-Archie criterion. A limited nine-sample dataset suggests m* increases with surface area. Links to NMR T1, flow units and Klinkenberg permeability are discussed.

## Impact of Clay Mineral Attributes in Estimating Damage in Carbonate Rocks

- ID: SCA2001-38, year: 2001
- Authors: W.H. Al-Bazzaz, Kisr Engler
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: clay migration, electron probe microanalysis, X-ray diffraction, mercury porosimetry, formation damage, carbonate reservoirs
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-38.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-38)

Combining XRD, electron microprobe mapping and mercury porosimetry on four low-permeability carbonate plugs shows clay size, location and distribution, not just amount, control clay migration damage.

Clay migration damages permeability in low-permeability carbonates, and the paper asks how clay attributes can be used to estimate that damage. Four carbonate plugs with different pore types and clay contents were characterized by XRD for clay type and abundance (kaolinite, illite), EPMA backscatter images and X-ray maps for clay size and location, mercury porosimetry to 33,000 psi, and critical velocity core floods. Plug 4 (micro-fractures, illite bridges, about 0.011 md) could not be intruded by mercury and showed clay blocking. Plug 12 had no clay. In Plug 19, most of the pore network (about 98%) had throats slightly larger than the clays, and permeability recovered at higher flood velocity. In Plug 24, illite/smectite clays were large enough to block small pore throats, causing significant impairment with only slight recovery. The authors conclude that clay composition, size, location and distribution, together with pore geometry, must be considered in addition to clay amount and type.

## An Integrated Approach to Compute Physical Properties of Core Samples

- ID: SCA2014-057, year: 2014
- Authors: S. Linden, T. Cvjetkovic, E. Glatt, A. Wiegmann
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: digital rock physics, SIMPLE-FFT Stokes solver, LIR-tree, Explicit Jump method, pore morphology drainage, Berea sandstone compression
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-057.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-057)

Five FFT-based and adaptive solvers compute permeability, conductivity, tortuosity, drainage, resistivity index and elasticity on one segmented Berea micro-CT image, including under compression, showing strong sensitivity to small compressions.

The paper presents an integrated digital rock workflow that computes many effective properties on the same segmented 3D image of Berea sandstone. Methods include SIMPLE-FFT and LIR-tree Stokes solvers, Explicit Jump for conduction and diffusion, pore morphology for drainage, and Lippmann-Schwinger for elasticity. Permeability falls from 107 mD to 18.8 mD with 3% compression, while the formation factor rises from 25.2 to 77, and tortuosity also increases. Permeability decreases quadratically and the other properties linearly with compression. The LIR solver needed 14 h and 5.8 GB versus 26.4 h and 40.5 GB for SIMPLE-FFT on a 16-core, 128 GB machine. Computed permeabilities fall in the middle of the benchmark range.

## Reservoir Properties of Conventional Rock: Routine Measurements on Mini-Plugs

- ID: SCA2014-081, year: 2014
- Authors: Dmitry Korobkov, Alexander Goncharov, Russian Federation
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: mini-plug, micro-CT, gas permeability, porosity measurement, steady-state relative permeability, upscaling
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 5
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-081.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-081)

Conventional porosimeter and permeameter equipment was adapted for 8 mm mini-plugs; porosity, permeability and Bentheimer relative permeability matched 30 mm plugs within uncertainty for homogeneous rocks.

Pore-scale simulation and micro-CT need mini-plugs, so routine and special core analysis methods must work at 5–10 mm diameter. A round robin on eight ceramic plugs across eight labs showed excellent porosimeter repeatability (±0.05%) but systematic differences up to ±0.4% and permeability discrepancies of 20% or more. Conventional equipment was modified with inserts, high-precision gauges and temperature stabilization to measure Ø8×8 mm mini-plugs. Eleven homogeneous plugs (porosity 5–29%, permeability 1–500 mD) were compared with mini-plugs drilled from them: porosity on mini-plugs was 0.11% higher on average, likely from surface vugs, and permeability showed no systematic deviation by steady-state or pressure falloff. Two-phase steady-state relative permeability on Bentheimer mini-plugs (about 30 mm long) matched Ø30 mm reference curves only after inlet and outlet mixers (stacked 100, 50 and 15 µm filters) suppressed slug flow.

## Determination of Physical Properties of Tight Porous Media Using Digital Core Physics/Analysis

- ID: SCA2015-052, year: 2015
- Authors: Shahin Ghomeshi, Saeed Taheri, Evgeny Skripkin, Sergey Kryuchkov, Apostolos Kantzas
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: SEM image reconstruction, tight rock, digital core physics, pore-scale simulation, upscaled permeability, NMR simulation
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2015/SCA2015-052.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2015-052)

Reconstructs 3-D tight rock from three SEM images and simulates porosity, permeability, resistivity and NMR, giving an upscaled permeability of about 6.9 µD.

The paper addresses estimating physical properties of tight rock from only three 2-D SEM images (5120×3828 pixels, ~637×476 µm). Small-grain permeable regions were cropped (33 images of 256×256 pixels) and interpolated into a 3-D pore structure, with no isotropy assumption. Stokes flow, Ohm's law and molecular diffusion were solved on the pore-scale mesh, giving porosity 0.09, resistivity 145 Ω.m, permeability about 3 µD in x and 2.3 µD in y, and an NMR spectrum. The medium and large grains were then traced and treated as impermeable in an upscaled Darcy model with average porosity 0.07. This gave an upscaled permeability of 6.9 µD. Results depend strongly on the threshold chosen, which was tuned to match a neighboring plug, and on resolution. Possible organic matter with nanopores would require adsorption and different governing equations.

## Modelling of Imbibition Relative Permeability by Direct Quasi-Static Approach

- ID: SCA2016-007, year: 2016
- Authors: S. Berg, M. Rücker, H. Ott, A. Georgiadis, H. van der Linde, F. Enzmann, M. Kersten, R. T. Armstrong, S. de With, J. Becker, A. Wiegmann
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: imbibition relative permeability, quasi-static morphological approach, synchrotron micro-CT, Euler characteristic, ganglion dynamics, connected pathway flow
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-007.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-007)

Quasi-static morphological modelling of imbibition on Gildehauser sandstone is compared with synchrotron micro-CT and steady-state data; it fails to reproduce fluid connectivity and relative permeability.

The study asks whether quasi-static pore-scale modelling is adequate for imbibition relative permeability, or whether dynamic approaches are needed. Imbibition in Gildehauser (Bentheimer-type) sandstone was imaged with fast synchrotron micro-CT (2.2 µm/voxel, 45 s per scan). Stokes flow on the segmented fluid distributions gave connected-pathway relative permeability, which matched steady-state SCAL data on a twin sample when plotted against mobile saturation. A morphological (erosion/dilation) quasi-static method matched MICP in drainage but gave very different imbibition relative permeability. The Euler characteristic shows the experimental non-wetting phase percolates up to Sw about 0.7-0.8, whereas the morphological model loses percolation at Sw about 0.05-0.35. The authors attribute this to missing cooperative, viscous-influenced events such as snap-off and coalescence, and the model behaves more like drainage.

## A Closer Look at Shale: Representative Elementary Volume Analysis with Laboratory 3D X-Ray Computed Microtomography and Nanotomography

- ID: SCA2011-58, year: 2011
- Authors: Jeff Gelb, Allen Gu, Tiffany Fong, Luke Hunter, S H Lau, Wenbing Yun Xradia
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: representative elementary volume, shale, X-ray nanotomography, watershed segmentation, digital core analysis, porosity
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-58.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-58)

Multi-scale lab micro- and nano-CT imaging of shale is used to determine a representative elementary volume for porosity, found to be about 17,000 µm³.
