Shale pores are hundreds of nanometers or smaller, so high-resolution imaging has a limited field of view, which makes representative elementary volume (REV) a key question. The authors combine lab micro-CT (VersaXRM) to locate regions of interest with nano-CT (UltraXRM, down to 50 nm resolution) on an extracted ~65 µm region of a shale plug. Images were segmented by watershed into pore, silicate matrix and inclusions, then sub-sampled into progressively smaller cubes to compute porosity versus analysis volume. Full-volume porosity at 150 nm resolution was 17.2%, matching ~17% from a crushed-sample measurement. Porosity and its standard deviation converged at about 17,000 µm³ (a cube ~25.7 µm per side), and the field of view holds roughly four such REVs.

## Enhanced pore space analysis by use of μ-CT, MIP, NMR, and SIP

- ID: SCA2017-086, year: 2017
- Authors: Zeyu Zhang, Sabine Kruschwitz, Andreas Weller, Matthias Halisch, Carsten Prinz
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: pore size distribution, mercury intrusion porosimetry, spectral induced polarization, NMR surface relaxivity, micro-CT, Bentheimer sandstone
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-086.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-086)

Pore size distributions of one Bentheimer sandstone from μ-CT, MIP, NMR and SIP are compared; body-to-throat radius ratio is about three when curves are resolution-adjusted to MIP.

Different pore-size methods have different resolution limits and sense either pore bodies or pore throats, which complicates comparison. One Bentheimer sandstone sample (porosity 23%, permeability 425 mD) was measured by μ-CT (1.75 µm voxel), MIP, 2 MHz NMR and SIP (0.002–100 Hz), and cumulative pore volume versus radius (Vc–r) curves were compared. MIP served as the fixed reference; μ-CT porosity was 0.184 versus 0.238 from MIP because pores below 1.75 µm radius are unresolved. Matching μ-CT and NMR medians gave a surface relaxivity of 54 µm/s. SIP resolved only radii of 0.1–10 µm and agreed fairly with MIP. Median radii give a pore body to throat ratio of about three, and fractal dimension in the 0.1–10 µm range was 2.678 (MIP) and 2.618 (SIP).

## Two-Phase Flow Properties Prediction from Small-Scale Data Using Pore-Network Modeling

- ID: SCA2001-16, year: 2001
- Authors: C. Laroche, O. Vizika, G. Hamon, R. Courtial
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore-network modeling, mercury injection capillary pressure, gas-oil relative permeability, pore-throat size distribution, body-throat correlation, fractal roughness
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-16.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-16)

Pore networks fitted to mercury Pc, porosity and permeability of three sandstones predict gas/oil Kr, but Pc alone cannot uniquely constrain transport properties.

The study tests whether gas/oil relative permeability can be predicted from mercury invasion capillary pressure curves alone using a 3-D pore-and-throat network model with coordination number 6. Networks were built for three water-wet sandstones (porosity 43%, ~21%, 19.6%; permeability from several Darcy down to 6.3 mD), varying body-throat size correlation, aspect ratio, periodicity length and fractal roughness to match Pc, porosity and permeability. Different geometric assumptions reproduced the same Pc curve and petrophysical data equally well, yet gave divergent Kr, especially for the wetting phase at low saturation. For Cores B and C, comparison with experimental Kr (including centrifuge and JBN data) was fairly good, best with body-throat correlation above 5 µm and uncorrelated sizes below. Correlated pore body and throat sizes seem to suit narrow size distributions, uncorrelated ones broad distributions; additional data on wall roughness and aspect ratio are needed.

## Two Examples of Adding Value Through Digital Rock Technology

- ID: SCA2012-18, year: 2012
- Authors: J. Schembre-McCabe, R. Salazar-Tio, J. Kamath
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: pore network modeling, micro-CT segmentation, drainage relative permeability, residual gas saturation, CO2 storage, pore size distribution
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-18.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-18)

Two digital rock applications: pore network modeling from micro-CT to interpret CO2 storage drainage and residual saturation data, and CT pore sizing of large pores in unconsolidated sands for drilling fluid design.

The paper shows business uses of digital rock beyond replacing experiments. In the first example, synchrotron micro-CT images (1.8 µm/pixel) of two clay-bearing sands (FZI ≥ 1.5) were segmented with SEM and QEMSCAN constraints, and pore network models reproduced mercury J-functions. Network-predicted drainage relative permeability showed higher gas endpoints and lower water saturation than endpoint and low-rate unsteady-state coreflood tests, which are suspected to suffer capillary end effects. Network results served as first guess for LET-based history matching, extending the steady-state curve, and trapped saturations matched countercurrent imbibition data. In the second example, 28 µm X-ray CT of unconsolidated sand, analyzed by local thickness and Feret diameter, revealed pores of 200-300 µm that MICP truncated, informing bridging particle size selection.

## Petrography-Guided Mineral Identification and Quantification - User Perspectives Based on a Multi-Laboratory Comparative Study of XRD, XRF, and FTIR

- ID: SCA2026-002, year: 2026
- Authors: Weijun Guo, Shouxiang Mark Ma, Jennifer Kharrazi
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: XRD mineral quantification, WD-XRF, FTIR, SEM-EDS petrography, elemental mass balance, inter-laboratory comparison
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2026/SCA2026-002.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2026-002)

Six outcrop rocks were analyzed by SEM-EDS, WD-XRF, XRD and FTIR across commercial labs; XRF-XRD elemental mass balance exposed Lab P's quartz overestimation in limestones.

End users of commercial mineralogy services face lab-dependent variability in XRD, XRF and FTIR results. Six outcrop samples (sandstone, granite, marble, dolomite, two limestones) were characterized by SEM-EDS petrography, WD-XRF at one lab, XRD at two labs and FTIR at one lab. An XRF-to-XRD elemental mass-balance check, using mineral end-point compositions, showed Lab P reported 1.4-2.4 wt% more quartz than XRF Si allows in near-pure calcite samples, with a matching calcite underestimate from normalization. Lab C was consistent with XRF in the limestones. Granite differences were attributed to sampling heterogeneity, and Mg in Kasota dolomite was overpredicted by both labs, suggesting end-point issues. Grain density differences between labs were only 0.002-0.004 g/cm3; FTIR identified mineral groups reproducibly but cannot quantify phases below about 3-5%. A workflow and QC checklist are proposed.

## Rock Typing and Petrophysical Property Estimation via Direct Analysis on Microtomographic Images

- ID: SCA2005-19, year: 2005
- Authors: F. Bauget, C. H. Arns, M. Saadatfar, M. L. Turner, A. P. Sheppard, R. M. Sok, W. V. Pinczewski, M.A. Knackstedt
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: micro-CT, Katz-Thompson, Kozeny-Carman, permeability correlation, critical pore diameter, digital rock physics
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-19.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-19)

Permeability correlations are tested on properties computed from micro-CT images of 36 cores; the Katz-Thompson critical pore diameter correlation works best across all rock types.

The paper tests empirical permeability correlations using petrophysical properties computed directly on 3D micro-CT images of 36 cores (homogeneous, unconsolidated and consolidated sands, a limestone and a vuggy carbonate), giving over 4000 independent data sets. Correlations tested are Kozeny-Carman, Katz-Thompson critical pore diameter, two NMR T2lm forms, and grain-size-based equations. The Katz-Thompson prefactor varies little (0.030 for limestone to 0.041 for homogeneous sands; 0.035 overall), giving the best fit and capturing permeability anisotropy. The NMR correlation with formation factor (Eqn. 4) fits better and has a more robust prefactor than the porosity-based form. Grain-size correlations agree within a factor of 2-3 for clean unconsolidated sands but overestimate permeability for silty soils.

## HOW MANY RELATIVE PERMEABILITY MEASUREMENTS DO YOU NEED? A Case Study from a North African Reservoir

- ID: SCA2002-03, year: 2002
- Authors: Khalifa Mohammed, Patrick Corbett
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: relative permeability sampling, hydraulic units, flow zone indicator, Winland R35, Lorenz plot, sector simulation
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-03.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-03)

Using hydraulic-unit rock typing, Lorenz plots and sector-model simulation of eight scenarios, the authors show four relative permeability curves from the key rock types suffice for a North African reservoir.

Relative permeability measurements are expensive and sample selection lacks formal guidelines. For a North African glacio-marine clastic reservoir, the authors classified core plug data into seven hydraulic units (flow zone indicator) and checked this against Winland R35 rock typing. Transmissive and storage units were identified from HU-coded Lorenz plots. Corey-regenerated laboratory curves were assigned to rock types in a sector simulation model under eight scenarios, using one to six curves. About 90% of oil production came through the transmissive units AHU1 and 2, while the storage units AHU3-6 held about 75% of oil in place. Using four curves from AHU1-4 gave field and well performance closest to the base case, and the existing sampling had concentrated on AHU4 and 5 with none in AHU1.

## Pore Structure Characterization of Core Samples from Daqing Oilfield by a Synthesis of Mercury Porosimetry and Image Analisys

- ID: SCA2005-22, year: 2005
- Authors: Hongxue HAN
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: mercury intrusion porosimetry, surface fractal dimension, two-point correlation function, pore size distribution, pore-to-throat aspect ratio, BSEM image analysis
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-22.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-22)

Combines mercury porosimetry and BSEM image analysis via fractal scaling to obtain pore size distributions and pore-to-throat aspect ratios for eight Daqing siltstones.

Pore size distributions spanning many length scales are hard to obtain from a single technique. The authors combine two-point correlation functions from backscatter SEM images with mercury intrusion porosimetry (MIP) on eight Daqing siltstone samples. Surface fractal dimensions from MIP and image analysis agree for most samples (e.g., D = 2.51 for sample 2). Scattering intensity computed from images was extrapolated to small sizes with the fractal power law and inverted with a polydisperse spherical pore model. The resulting pore volume distribution spans five orders of magnitude and overlaps the low-pressure mercury extrusion curve. A constant apparent pore-to-throat aspect ratio of about 2–4 brings the pore volume distribution and MIP curves into reasonable coincidence. Seven of eight samples show similar pore structure, sample 8 being the exception.

## High Resolution µ-CT Combined to Numerical Models to Assess Electrical Properties of Bimodal Carbonates

- ID: SCA2008-37, year: 2008
- Authors: S. Youssef, M. Han, D. Bauer, E. Rosenberg, S. Bekri, M. Fleury, O. Vizika
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: micro-CT, pore network model, resistivity index, formation factor, bimodal carbonates, microporosity
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 8
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-37.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-37)

Three-phase segmentation of µ-CT images feeds a dual-porosity pore network model that reproduces formation factor and resistivity index of bimodal carbonates, with microporosity placement controlling RI curvature.

Electrical properties of carbonates are hard to simulate because of unresolved microporosity. The authors imaged Fontainebleau sandstone, Estaillade and Lavoux limestones at 3 µm voxel size, optimized acquisition (3600 projections, 0.1 mm Cu filter) and segmented images into solid, microporosity and macropores. A macropore network was built with microporosity acting in parallel along a fraction of throats (p_seg) with a conductivity length l_con. For Fontainebleau, PNM and random-walk FF (about 13) were slightly above experiment (11.5), and film conductance was needed to reproduce the RI bending down at low Sw. For Estaillade, p_seg of 0.56 (near the image-derived contact surface of 47%) reproduced the experimental RI double curvature, while for Lavoux, with more microporosity, l_con controlled RI and matched the near-Archie behaviour (n about 1.9), though FF was overestimated.

## Focussed Ion Beam Assisted Three-Dimensional Rock Imaging at Submicron Scale

- ID: SCA2003-47, year: 2003
- Authors: Liviu Tomutsa, Velimir Radmilovic
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: focused ion beam, serial sectioning, 3D pore structure, chalk, diatomite, submicron imaging
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-47.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-47)

A focused ion beam serial-sectioning method is developed to reconstruct 3D pore structure of diatomite and chalk at submicron resolution, with clean surfaces and no apparent distortion.

Micron-scale X-ray microtomography cannot resolve the pore structure of fine-grained rocks such as chalk and diatomite. The authors developed a serial sectioning method using a dual-beam FEI DB235 FIB/SEM, milling thin layers with Ga+ ions (up to 30 kV, up to 20,000 pA) and imaging each fresh surface with secondary electrons or ions. Samples need flat, rectangular preparation, mounting on 45° stubs and carbon coating; images are aligned using milled markers, thresholded and stacked to build 3D volumes. Tested on cleaned Belridge diatomite (sections 0.2 µm apart) and epoxy-impregnated North Sea chalk (0.1 µm apart), milling gave flat surfaces with high pore-grain contrast and no apparent boundary distortion. Epoxy impregnation gave better contrast and easier thresholding than the unimpregnated diatomite, where brightness variations inside pores required more image processing.

## Heterogenous Carbonates – Integrating Plug and Whole Core Data Using Rock Types

- ID: SCA2012-12, year: 2012
- Authors: Mark Skalinski, Rafael Salazar, Gerry LaTorraca, Zheng Yang, John Urbach
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: petrophysical rock types, carbonate heterogeneity, Lattice Boltzmann permeability, Darcy upscaling, whole core CT, representative elementary volume
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 3
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-12.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-12)

Petrophysical rock types from CT, mini-permeability and resistivity guide plug selection in heterogeneous carbonates; Darcy upscaling of Lattice-Boltzmann permeabilities matches full-volume results within 10%.

Multiscale heterogeneity in Middle East Eocene dolomitized carbonates (Wafra field) raises the question of plug representativeness. Five whole core pieces were CT imaged, and 9 full-diameter samples were measured for porosity and permeability, then slabbed for 1 cm grid mini-permeability and resistivity mapping. K-means clustering of CT-calibrated porosity, permeability and resistivity gave five petrophysical rock types, used to choose 26 plug locations. Full-diameter permeability was about half an order of magnitude higher than plug values, indicating a permeability REV larger than 1 inch. For upscaling, Lattice-Boltzmann permeabilities of 216^3 sub-volumes (6x6x4 grid) fed a Darcy solver, giving 171 mD versus 193 mD for the full LB simulation and 223 mD for the plug, within 10% of the full-volume value.

## Pore-Network Modeling of Rock Transport Properties: Application to a Carbonate

- ID: SCA2006-22, year: 2006
- Authors: S. Békri, O. Vizika
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network model, formation resistivity factor, Weibull pore-throat distribution, resistivity index, relative permeability, mercury capillary pressure
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-22.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-22)

3D pore-network models of a homogeneous carbonate show that matching formation factor, in addition to Pc, porosity and permeability, selects the pore-throat distribution that predicts resistivity index and relative permeability.

Fitting mercury capillary pressure, porosity and permeability does not give a unique pore network, so predictions of transport properties are uncertain. The authors build cubic-lattice pore-throat networks (coordination 6, aspect ratio 4, L=70 µm) for a homogeneous unimodal carbonate (porosity 34.1%, permeability 2.7 mD, FF=7.54). Pore-throat size distributions are tuned from the mercury interpretation and approximated by Weibull functions with different pore-volume exponents. Several distributions fit Pc equally well but give FF from about 7.5 to 16.1. Only the one matching the measured FF (λp=0.55, within 1%) also reproduces the resistivity index and the oil/water relative permeabilities of a restored-wettability composite core. A Weibull sensitivity study shows α (average throat size) strongly affects Pc, resistivity index and saturation exponent, while β (width) affects low-saturation resistivity behavior.

## Permeability Measurements for Two Middle

- ID: SCA2004-34, year: 2004
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: unsteady state waterflood, steady state relative permeability, in-situ saturation monitoring, carbonate wettability, imbibition capillary pressure, reservoir condition SCAL
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2004/SCA2004-34.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2004-34)

Reservoir-condition unsteady and steady state waterfloods with in-situ saturation monitoring on two Middle Eastern carbonates give consistent relative permeability, low residual oil and mixed-wet behaviour.

Old, limited SCAL data for two Middle Eastern carbonate reservoirs (0.5-3 mD) prompted new water/oil relative permeability measurements at full reservoir conditions (about 4000 psi, 120°C) after live oil ageing. Six plug and one 52 cm composite USS floods were run for Reservoir 1, and one USS plus one SS flood on a 27 cm composite for Reservoir 2, with gamma-attenuation in-situ saturation monitoring. Data were interpreted with dyrectSCAL simulation to correct for capillary end effects and to derive imbibition Pc. Breakthrough occurred late (~0.6 PV) with 60-76% displacement efficiency, and residual oil at the highest rate was 12-23% PV. End-point Krw was 0.29-0.47 for Reservoir 1 and 0.61 for Reservoir 2. SS and USS results agreed closely (ROS within 2.3%), and Amott/USBM indices showed intermediate to oil-wet samples, with RRT3 the most intermediate-wet.

## Pore scale investigation of dopants impact on wettability alteration

- ID: SCA2024_1025, year: 2024
- Authors: Franck Nono, Titly Farhana Faisal, Florent Fischer, Fabrice Pairoys, Mohamed Regaieg, Cyril Caubit
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: dopants, wettability alteration, micro-CT, Amott-like experiment, spontaneous imbibition, pore network modeling
- Cited by 3 papers in the corpus, cites 4
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2024/SCA2024_1025.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2024_1025)

Two micro-CT Amott-like experiments on one sandstone plug, aged with doped versus non-doped brine, show dopants speed capillary rise, increase oil trapping and alter wettability outputs.

Iodide dopants used for micro-CT contrast may alter wettability, questioning digital rock wettability anchoring protocols. The authors ran two Amott-like experiments on the same 10 mm × 15 mm semi-unconsolidated sandstone mini plug (4.65 D, 21.4% porosity). Ageing used doped brine (40 g/l NaI) in experiment 1 and non-doped formation brine in experiment 2, with matching initial Swi (15.4% vs 15.6%). Both showed a mixed-wet-small wettability model, but with doped brine capillary rise was at least twice as fast, less brine imbibed in the macro-porosity (about 10 saturation points less), residual oil was about 10 p.u. higher, and some spontaneous drainage occurred only with doped brine. The non-doped ageing protocol gives more reliable wettability inputs for pore network simulation, though it is more time-consuming and limited for samples with significant sub-porosity; doping the oil is proposed as a future alternative.

## Pore Network Modeling of Experimental Capillary Pressure Hysteresis Relationships

- ID: SCA2013-015, year: 2013
- Authors: Behrooz Raeesi, Norman Morrow, Geoffrey Mason
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network modeling, capillary pressure hysteresis, snap-off, residual non-wetting phase trapping, Bentheim sandstone, scanning curves
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2013/SCA2013-015.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2013-015)

A pore network model was tuned to Bentheim sandstone capillary pressure hysteresis data; restricting snap-off in short throats (L/D<π) matched residual trapping and predicted scanning curves.

The study tests a pore network model (the LOB network, 38,495 elements) against detailed air/brine capillary pressure hysteresis data for Bentheim sandstone, including multiple scanning curves. The original network gave too little hysteresis (PT/PE 0.87 versus 0.53 measured) and too much trapped non-wetting phase. Raising aspect ratio and adjusting pore size distribution and spatial correlation improved hysteresis but residual NWP stayed high (54% versus 35-43% measured). Disallowing snap-off in throats with length/diameter below π cut snap-offs from 97% to 47% of displacements and matched the residual saturation of 35%. Without further adjustment the tuned model predicted scanning loops within 1-2% trapping, and its trapped blob size distribution agreed with synchrotron images of Berea reported by Prodanović et al.

## Pore Characterisation, Relating Mini-Permeability and CT-Scan Porosity of Carbonate Cores

- ID: SCA2005-72, year: 2005
- Authors: M.T. Tweheyo, A.S. Lackner, G.K. Andersen, J.K. Ringen, O. Torsaeter
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: mini-permeameter, CT-scan porosity, mercury porosimetry, bimodal pore size distribution, carbonate heterogeneity, SEM-EDS mineralogy
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-72.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-72)

Mini-permeameter data overlaid on CT porosity of two carbonate cores showed no useful permeability-porosity correlation; mercury porosimetry, thin sections and SEM showed heterogeneous, bimodal pore systems.

The study tests whether permeability and porosity can be related at several sampling scales in two heterogeneous reservoir carbonate cores. Mini-permeameter grids (10x10 on end faces) were overlaid on matching CT porosity images (414x414 values) and compared with helium porosity and air permeability from whole cores, SCAL plugs and 1-inch plugs. Permeability-porosity plots were random with large scatter, and averaging CT porosity to coarser grids did not improve correlation. Mercury porosimetry showed large hysteresis and bimodal pore size distributions, with core 1 having larger pores and wider distribution (lambda 0.53-0.61) than core 2 (0.62-0.69). Thin sections and SEM/EDS showed core 1 as a calcite-rich bioclastic packstone with calcite cement on pore walls, and core 2 as strongly dolomitized (about 7-8 wt% Mg) and more homogeneous.

## Petrophysics, Image Analysis, and Sample Size

- ID: SCA2007_54, year: 2007
- Authors: Christopher M. Prince
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: thin section image analysis, representative sample size, optical porosity, packing flaws, permeability prediction, pixel resolution
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_54.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_54)

Thin-section image analysis of porosity needs samples of several millimeters or more at high resolution; small images give unreliable porosity and petrophysical predictions.

Image analysis relating pore networks to petrophysical properties is stochastic and needs large samples with many pore bodies, but fixed pixel dimensions force a trade-off between resolution and sample size. The author subsampled thin-section images of two unlithified Gulf of Mexico sands (1.9 µm) and a Fontainebleau sandstone (4.4 µm), covering about 1.6 cm squares, and computed the standard deviation of optical porosity versus subsample size. Standard deviation fell below 1 porosity unit only at 3-5 mm for the sands, and never below 1.5 PU for Fontainebleau. The curves flatten near 1 mm (about 5 grain diameters) but vary out to 5 mm, indicating larger multi-grain fabric elements such as well-packed clusters (~1 mm) separated by packing flaws. For Fontainebleau, variability of the packing-flaw network extends to at least 6 mm, so 512² to 2048² pixel images at these resolutions may be inadequate.

## A New Approach for the Characterization of the Pore Structure of Dual Porosity Rocks

- ID: SCA2006-24, year: 2006
- Authors: Christos D. Tsakiroglou, Marios A. Ioannidis, Edison Armitharaj, Olga Vizika
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: mercury intrusion porosimetry, dual porosity, BSEM image analysis, percolation theory, accessibility functions, critical path analysis
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-24.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-24)

Combines BSEM images, mercury porosimetry and inverse percolation modeling to estimate pore body/throat distributions and accessibility functions of dual porosity rocks, then predicts permeability and formation factor via critical path analysis.

Dual porosity rocks span many length scales that no single technique covers. The authors fuse BSEM image autocorrelation with the surface fractal dimension from high-pressure Hg intrusion to derive a volume-based pore body radius distribution, fitted as macro- and micro-porosity components. Percolation-based analytical models of Hg intrusion and retraction are inverted to estimate throat size distribution and drainage and imbibition accessibility functions for two carbonates (cj4, st-maximin) and a sandstone (goui). The models reproduce the intrusion and retraction curves; macropores are far fewer than micropores, and carbonate micropores show a linear DAF consistent with a fractal-like cascade. Critical path analysis gave permeabilities of 30.2 mD (cj4), 2189 mD (st-maximin) and 174.7 mD (goui), with formation factors of 51.5, 6.55 and 17.8.

## A Fully Coupled Geochemical Model with a Pore Scale Lattice Boltzmann Simulator - Principles and First Results

- ID: SCA2010-14, year: 2010
- Authors: Aksel Hiorth, Espen Jettestuen, Lawrence M. Cathles, Reidar I. Korsnes, Merete V. Madland
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: lattice Boltzmann, reactive transport, calcite dissolution, magnesite precipitation, surface charge and potential, chalk MgCl2 flooding
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2010/SCA2010-14.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2010-14)

A lattice Boltzmann pore-scale simulator coupled with a geochemical model reproduces MgCl2 flooding effluent data in chalk, with magnesite inhibition of calcite dissolution improving the match.

The paper presents a 2D lattice Boltzmann reactive transport simulator coupled to a geochemical solver that handles aqueous speciation, surface complexation, and rate-controlled mineral dissolution and precipitation at pore walls. The chemical solver was checked against PHREEQC for a calcite-dolomite-MgCl2 case at 50°C, falling within the spread of PHREEQC results from different databases. The model was applied to published Liege chalk flooding with 0.219 M MgCl2 at 130°C and 1 PV/day, using a tube bundle geometry matched on Péclet number (8). Fitting only a rate constant (k=2.8e-3/min) matched the steady effluent trends, but the early calcium peak was missed, leaving about 0.004 mol Ca unexplained; adsorption (about 0.00066 mol sites) could not account for it. Adding fast-dissolving calcite (4%) reproduced the peak, while magnesite-dependent inhibition of calcite dissolution captured the transient better. The model also gives surface charge, potential and adsorbed Mg/Ca profiles along the core.

## Porosity and Permeability Analysis on Nanoscale FIB-SEM 3D Imaging of Shale Rock

- ID: SCA2011-30, year: 2011
- Authors: Shawn Zhang
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: FIB-SEM, shale porosity, image segmentation, top-hat filter, Stokes flow permeability, organic matter pores
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-30.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-30)

FIB-SEM 3D images of a shale at 5 nm are segmented for organic matter and pores, and Stokes flow simulation gives permeability; porosity depends strongly on segmentation.

Shale-gas mudstones have nanoscale pores that are hard to characterize, so a North American mudstone sample (about 9.9 x 8.6 x 5.4 µm) was imaged by FIB-SEM at 5 nm resolution. Organic matter and pore networks were segmented; the organic matter network was almost fully percolating (24.1% of 24.2% non-mineral volume). Simple thresholding made porosity highly sensitive (about 4% to 13% for thresholds 45 to 65), while top-hat segmentation reduced this sensitivity; combining a low threshold with top-hat gave 6.25% porosity. A new Stokes solver (Avizo XLab), validated against analytical cases and Kozeny-Carman, gave pore-network permeabilities of 280-936 nD and organic-matter-network permeabilities of about 100-224 µD. The authors consider these underestimates because pore throats below 10-15 nm and diffusion are not captured, and laboratory validation is underway.

## The application of IR spectroscopy for real time mineralogical analyses of core and cuttings

- ID: SCA2014-050, year: 2014
- Authors: Gavin Hunt, Technical Director
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: SWIR imaging spectroscopy, clay mineralogy, SpecCam, mineral logging, illite permeability, hydrocarbon mapping
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-050.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-050)

Shows how the SpecCam SWIR imaging spectrometer maps clays, carbonates and hydrocarbons continuously on core and cuttings, with North Sea case studies on correlation and permeability.

Point-sampling mineral methods such as XRD and point counting are slow, need sample preparation and give sparse data. The paper describes the SpecCam short-wave infrared (1.3-2.5 micron) imaging reflectance spectrometer, which scans core and cuttings at 0.5 mm pixels (about 28,000 points per metre) without preparation. It is semi-quantitative, cannot identify quartz, and cannot be calibrated for hydrocarbons. In a Northern North Sea field, data from 11 wells distinguished dickite-bearing Formation B from formations T and N, which differed in illite type, and this led to revised formation boundaries and well correlations. In a Rotliegend gas reservoir, illite content was inversely related to permeability (about 6 mD above the clay baffle). Below the baffle permeability averaged about 0.1 mD with lower illite, which suggests chlorite is responsible. Hydrocarbon spectral features in Brent core also revealed compositional differences across a calcite seal.

## Gas-water steady-state relative permeability determination with two approaches; experimental and digital rock analysis, strengths and weaknesses

- ID: SCA2016-012, year: 2016
- Authors: R. Farokhpoor, E. Westphal, N. Idowu, P.E. Øren, B. Fletcher
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: digital rock analysis, pore network modelling, steady-state relative permeability, process-based model, micro-CT, capillary end effect
- Cited by 2 papers in the corpus, cites 4
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-012.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-012)

Gas-water imbibition relative permeability from steady-state experiments on three sandstones is compared with pore network modelling on micro-CT sub-plugs and process-based models, showing good agreement in fractional flow.

The paper compares steady-state gas-water imbibition relative permeability from coreflood experiments with digital rock predictions for three sandstone plugs (0.17-0.29 porosity, 1.5-2162 mD). Experiments showed strong saturation non-uniformity, which was corrected by history matching with the Sendra simulator, a non-unique approach. Pore networks were extracted from micro-CT sub-plugs (1.5-5.12 µm/voxel) and from process-based models built from BSEM images, and two-phase flow was simulated with a pore network model. The PBM matched MICP capillary pressure well for all plugs, while sub-plug models were limited by resolution, notably in the clay-rich tight plug 2 (Swi=0.48). Fractional flow curves differed by 1-4 saturation units at breakthrough, though gas relative permeability showed large discrepancies, possibly due to saturation non-uniformity.

## Pore Scale Characterization of Carbonates at Multiple Scales: Integration of Micro-CT, Bsem and Fibsem

- ID: SCA2009-18, year: 2009
- Authors: Robert M. Sok, Trond Varslot, Abid Ghous, Shane Latham, Adrian P. Sheppard, Mark A. Knackstedt
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: micro-CT, FIBSEM, image registration, microporosity, carbonate pore network, SEM dewarping
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2009/SCA2009-18.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2009-18)

Registers micro-CT, dewarped SEM and FIBSEM images of carbonates across scales, correlating CT attenuation with SEM microporosity and pore size to build dual-scale pore networks.

Carbonate pore structure spans nanometers to centimeters, so no single image captures it. The authors image plugs by micro-CT (20 to 1.5 micron voxels), then thin sections or stubs by BSEM (0.25 to 1.24 micron pixels) and FIBSEM (~50-105 nm), and register them in 3D. SEM images are dewarped using a chromium-disk template to remove scanning distortion. CT attenuation in the microporous phase correlates linearly with SEM porosity, and with local SEM pore size, allowing microporosity and pore size to be mapped through the CT volume. For a mudstone, FIBSEM gave porosity 33.8% (plug 29.3%) and permeability 2-3 mD (plug 1 mD). For a multimodal sample, CT macroporosity of 11% plus microporosity of 24% gave 35% total (MICP 36%), and a FIBSEM network embedded stochastically into the CT volume produced a connected dual-scale network.

## Applicability of Carman-Kozeny Equation for Well Consolidated Samples

- ID: SCA2013-080, year: 2013
- Authors: Babak Salimifard, Douglas Ruth, Craig Lindsay, Core Specialist, Mark Allen, West Africa
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: Carman-Kozeny equation, permeability prediction, grain size distribution, tortuosity, effective grain diameter, mercury injection porosimetry
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2013/SCA2013-080.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2013-080)

Tests Carman-Kozeny permeability prediction from grain size on 25 consolidated Ghana sandstones; it scatters widely and performs worse than the MIP-based Ruth et al. method.

The Carman-Kozeny (C-K) equation was developed for sand packs, and this study tests it on 25 well consolidated samples from a Turonian formation offshore Ghana, with porosity of 3-28%. Permeability was estimated from grain size distributions using C-K with and without a porosity-based tortuosity term (tau^2 = 1/phi), and with three effective grain diameters: D10, harmonic mean and Mastera's recursive method. Including tortuosity improved the fit, and D10 tracked measured values best even though the other diameters gave higher R2. Compared against the MIP-based Ruth et al. method on the same samples, C-K showed significant scatter and large deviations from measured permeability. The poor performance may reflect the model's inapplicability or the dependence of grain size data on the energy used to disaggregate the samples.

## Multi-Scale Permeability Trends Using Digital Rock Physics

- ID: SCA2014-043, year: 2014
- Authors: R. Sungkorn, V.A. Gundepalli, Y. Mu, A. Grader, C. Sisk, S. Bhakta, N. Derzhi, J. Dvorkin, J. Toelke
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: digital rock physics, permeability upscaling, building blocks, lattice Boltzmann, Brinkman-Darcy, porosity-permeability trend
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-043.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-043)

A digital rock workflow upscales porosity-permeability trends from building blocks to larger scales; on artificial rocks, ignoring within-block variation gives 20-42% permeability error.

Heterogeneous reservoir rocks span many length scales, so petrophysical properties must be upscaled consistently. The authors propose a workflow that identifies rock-fabric building blocks from coarse-scale images, derives porosity-permeability and formation factor trends from 50-300 digital subdivisions of high-resolution images of each block, and populates the coarse image voxels with these trends. Flow is solved with the Stokes equation at the finest scale and the Brinkman-extended Darcy equation at larger scales, using GPU lattice Boltzmann simulation. Tests used two artificial 1 mm fine samples (power-law exponents 3.2 and 7.3) combined into 25 mm models in series, parallel and diagonal arrangements. Ignoring porosity variation within blocks overpredicted permeability by 20% (parallel), 29% (diagonal) and 42% (series). The workflow can generate trends with up to two orders of magnitude fewer samples than laboratory methods, and the authors recommend pairing it with a few laboratory measurements.

## Assessment of Sidewall Cores for Routine and Special Core Analyses

- ID: SCA2015-035, year: 2015
- Authors: F. Pairoys, A. Nadeev, K. Kirkman, G. Poole, K. Bohn, M. Alexander, N. Radwan, W. Abdallah, M. Akbar
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: sidewall coring, micro-CT, mercury injection capillary pressure, NMR T2, Klinkenberg permeability, formation factor
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2015/SCA2015-035.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2015-035)

Twin plugs from Berea and Indiana blocks cut with a new 1.5-inch sidewall coring tool and a lab plugger gave comparable petrophysical results, indicating no coring damage.

Sidewall cores have lacked acceptance for core analysis because of non-standard size and possible coring alteration. A new rotary sidewall tool cuts 1.5-inch diameter plugs, and this study compares them with twin plugs from a conventional lab plugging machine. Plugs were cut from Berea sandstone (500-800 mD) and Indiana limestone (15-150 mD) blocks, selected from minipermeameter grids at similar permeability. Helium porosity, grain density, Klinkenberg permeability, micro-CT, MICP, brine permeability, formation factor and NMR T2 were measured. Sidewall and lab plugs agreed, e.g. cementation exponent about 2.0 for sandstone and 2.1 for limestone, with matching MICP and T2 distributions and no evidence of induced fractures. The authors conclude the sidewall cores suit RCA and SCAL, with SCAL work in progress.

## Multi-Scale Imaging and Modeling Workflow to Capture and Characterize Microporosity in Sandstone

- ID: SCA2013-013, year: 2013
- Authors: Long H, Nardi C, Idowu N, Carnerup A, Øren P.E, Knackstedt M, Varslot T, Sok R.M. Lithicon
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: dry/wet difference mapping, micro-CT, microporosity, tight gas sand, SEM-EDS mineralogy, hybrid upscaling
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 6
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2013/SCA2013-013.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2013-013)

A multi-scale micro-CT/SEM workflow using dry/wet difference mapping captures sub-resolution microporosity in sandstone and tight gas sand, matching lab porosity and giving better permeability than forced porosity matching.

Micro-CT porosity is limited by resolution, especially in clay-rich and tight gas sands with sub-resolution pores. The authors present a five-stage workflow combining plug- and pore-scale micro-CT, dry/wet difference mapping, registered SEM and SEM-EDS mineralogy, FIBSEM nanoscale models, and hybrid upscaling. For a conventional sandstone micro-plug (3.5 µm/voxel, lab MICP porosity 21.7%), dry-only segmentation gave 18.3% while dry/wet difference mapping gave 21.8%. Forcing porosity by thresholding gave a 22% larger median critical diameter and a Lattice-Boltzmann permeability of 1400 mD versus 570 mD for difference mapping, against ~450 mD measured. The method was also applied to a 0.1 mD tight gas sand to map microporous kaolinite and derive drainage capillary pressure and relative permeability curves.

## A Multi-Scale, Image-Based Pore Network Modeling Approach to Simulate Two-Phase Flow in Heterogeneous Rocks

- ID: SCA2015-027, year: 2015
- Authors: Tom Bultreys, Wesley De Boever, Luc Van Hoorebeke, Veerle Cnudde
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: dual pore network model, microporosity, micro-CT, drainage relative permeability, resistivity index, carbonate
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 3
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2015/SCA2015-027.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2015-027)

A dual pore network model couples micro-CT-resolved macropores with continuum microporosity via micro-links to simulate drainage Pc, Kr and RI in Estaillades and Savonnières limestones, matching data better than single-scale PNM.

Modeling multiphase flow in rocks with broad pore size distributions is hard because of the resolution/sample-size trade-off. The authors extract a macropore network from micro-CT images (Estaillades 3.1 µm voxels, Savonnières 3.8 µm voxels) and connect macropores through unresolved microporous clusters using 'micro-links' carrying upscaled properties (porosity, Pc, Kr, permeability, formation factor, breakthrough pressure). Drainage simulations with a modified quasi-static Imperial College simulator give Pc, Kr and RI curves that match experimental trends better than single-scale PNM, including double-bending RI and draining of hollow ooliths (30% of macropore space in Savonnières, starting at Sw = 0.61). Sensitivity tests show that higher microporous permeability lowers Kr,nw and raises Kr,w, breakthrough saturation matters little, and scaling microporous throat sizes strongly affects the invasion sequence. Remaining challenges are the user-defined micro-link cut-off length and the difficulty of obtaining microporosity input parameters.

## Reconstruction of Multi-Scale Heterogeneous Porous Media and Their Flow Prediction

- ID: SCA2007_16, year: 2007
- Authors: K. Wu, Z. Jiang, G. D. Couples, M.I.J. Van Dijke, K.S. Sorbie
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore architecture models, Markov random field reconstruction, sphere-fitting pore size, invasion percolation, mercury injection capillary pressure, multi-scale porosity
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_16.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_16)

Markov-mesh 3D reconstructions from thin sections at different resolutions, analysed with sphere-fitting, give pore networks and simulated mercury injection curves that can be combined for multi-scale rocks.

Multi-scale rocks such as siltstone and carbonate are hard to reconstruct because macropores and micropores cannot be captured in one small voxel cube. The authors build 3D pore architecture models (PAMs) from thin-section images with a Markov random field method, using separate images at different resolutions for each scale. Eight samples (sandstone to mudstone) are reconstructed, and simulated permeabilities agree reasonably with measured values (e.g. 2236 vs 2500 mD; 71.5 vs 82.1 mD). A non-overlapping sphere-fitting tool extracts pore size distributions, showing mean pore diameters of about 0.6 µm (submicron) versus 2.5 µm (micron scale) in siltstone, and 0.8 versus 4.5 µm in carbonate. Invasion percolation mercury injection curves from the two scales are distinct, and the end of the coarse curve coincides with the start of the fine curve, so they can be combined into a composite MICP curve.

## Validation of Methods for Multi-Scale Pore Space Reconstruction and Their Use in Prediction of Flow Properties of Carbonate

- ID: SCA2008-34, year: 2008
- Authors: K. Wu, A. Ryazanov, M.I.J. van Dijke, Z. Jiang, J. Ma, K.S. Sorbie
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore architecture model, thin section reconstruction, self-reconstruction, pore network extraction, multi-scale carbonate, two-phase relative permeability
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-34.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-34)

Pore architecture model reconstructions from 2D thin sections are validated by self-reconstruction of a sandstone, then multi-scale carbonate reconstructions are combined, showing micropores affect network-predicted flow.

The paper tests a Markov random field based pore architecture model (PAM) that reconstructs 3D pore space from 2D thin sections, with pore network extraction and two-phase network flow simulation. Validation uses two-cycle self-reconstruction of a 300^3 voxel, 5.6 µm sandstone CT image. Network permeability fell from 2509 mD to 2046 mD (-18%) after the first cycle and 1514 mD after the second, with pore size and shape factor distributions close but with a small loss of the smallest pores; relative permeabilities agreed well. For a carbonate, coarse (1.33 µm) and fine (0.13 µm) reconstructions were combined by refining and superimposing the pore phases. The combined model raised permeability and connectivity (max coordination number 21 to 89 in one case) and changed relative permeability relative to the macropore-only model, indicating micropores matter. Comparison with experimental carbonate data is left for future work.

## Understanding Imbibition Data in Complex Carbonate Rock Types

- ID: SCA2014-059, year: 2014
- Authors: Moustafa Dernaika, Zubair Kalam, Svein Skjaeveland
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: imbibition relative permeability, Corey exponents, steady-state fractional flow, carbonate rock typing, mixed-wet, pore-throat size distribution
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-059.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-059)

Steady-state imbibition Kr on mixed-wet Abu Dhabi carbonates varies systematically with rock type and permeability; Corey exponent differences reflect pore geometry, not wettability.

Carbonate SCAL relative permeability data are often left unexplained because rock types are poorly characterized. Sixteen 1.5-inch plugs from two Abu Dhabi fields were typed using thin sections, MICP pore-throat size distributions, poro-perm and CT images. Steady-state imbibition Kr was measured at full reservoir conditions with live fluids after three weeks of aging, with in-situ saturation monitoring and simulation history matching. Higher-permeability samples gave higher Krw, lower Kro, higher Krwmax and higher average Sor, with larger differences between average and inlet Sor. Corey exponents rose for no (e.g. 4.7 at 392 mD vs 2.9 at 0.81 mD) and fell for nw (1.8 vs 4.3). Because capillary pressure showed similar mixed- to oil-wet behaviour in all samples, these trends are attributed to pore geometry, accessibility and surface area rather than wettability.

## Multi-Scale Rock Analysis for Improved Rock Typing in Complex Carbonates

- ID: SCA2015-034, year: 2015
- Authors: Moustafa Dernaika, Yasir Naseer Uddin, Safouh Koronfol, Osama Al Jallad, Gary Sinclair
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: dual energy CT, rock typing, carbonate dolomitization, effective atomic number, MICP pore-throat size, micro-CT upscaling
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2015/SCA2015-034.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2015-034)

Dual energy whole-core CT identifies calcite and dolomitized lithologies, which are linked to plug poro-perm, MICP and micro/nano CT to define and upscale carbonate rock types.

Carbonate heterogeneity makes plug-scale rock types hard to upscale to whole core. Three hundred feet of core were dual energy CT scanned, with bulk density and Zeff computed every 0.5 mm to log calcite (66 vol%), dolomite (15%) and partially dolomitized (19%) intervals, confirmed by XRD. Representative plugs showed two poro-perm trends: calcite at 0.02-4 mD and dolomite/partially dolomitized at 5-1000 mD, so dolomitization raised permeability by 2-3 orders of magnitude. MICP, thin sections and micro to nano CT (0.064-4 µm/voxel) defined pore geometry for each rock type, with mercury peaks at 0.7, 3 and 10 µm. CT-derived porosity and permeability logs matched plug data well, with better porosity agreement below 15% where the core is less heterogeneous.

## Resolving the Link between Porosity and Permeability in Carbonate Pore Systems

- ID: SCA2017-077, year: 2017
- Authors: Moustafa R Dernaika, Gary G Sinclair
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: carbonate texture, porosity-permeability relationship, thin-section petrography, dual energy CT, capillary pressure, rock typing
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-077.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-077)

Classifying over 500 Middle East carbonate plugs by texture (grainy, mixed, muddy) resolves scattered porosity-permeability data into distinct trends and capillary pressure behaviors.

Permeability prediction from porosity in carbonates is poor because of complex, multimodal pore systems, with permeability varying three to five orders of magnitude at a given porosity. Over 500 1.5-inch plugs from five Middle East limestone reservoirs (porosity ~5-30%, permeability ~0.01-1000 mD) were statistically selected using dual-energy CT. Helium porosity and gas permeability were combined with texture classification from thin-section photomicrographs. Grainy samples gave the highest permeability and lowest entry pressures, muddy samples the lowest permeability, and mixed samples fell between. The extent of each trend was controlled by compaction, cementation and dissolution, and similar textures in different reservoirs gave similar permeability ranges. Texture therefore supports permeability prediction and rock typing.

## Characteristics of Carbonate Rock Types in the Middle East

- ID: SCA2018-065, year: 2018
- Authors: Moustafa Dernaika, Bashar Mansour, Osama Al Jallad, Abu Dhabi
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: carbonate rock typing, mercury injection capillary pressure, pore-throat size distribution, Dunham texture, diagenesis, porosity-permeability trends
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2018/SCA2018-065.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2018-065)

Over 1000 plugs from seven Middle East carbonate reservoirs are classified by texture and diagenesis into 25 rock types linked to poroperm trends and MICP curves.

Carbonate rock typing is difficult because of heterogeneity, so the authors built a texture- and diagenesis-based scheme. More than 1000 plugs from seven Middle East reservoirs were characterized with helium porosity, gas permeability, thin sections, high-pressure MICP and dual-energy plug CT used to screen for fractures and select representative trims. Textures (grainy, mixed, muddy) each gave a distinct porosity-permeability trend, with diagenesis (cementation, compaction, dissolution) controlling the extent along each trend. Within each trend, detailed Dunham textures and diagenesis defined 25 rock types (12 grainy, 8 mixed, 5 muddy), tabulated with porosity, permeability, pore-throat size, entry pressure and Swi. For example, rudstone-boundstone has ~1000 mD with a ~100 micron PTSD peak and 0.1 psi entry pressure, whereas muddy rocks have permeability below 10 mD, entry pressure above 10 psi and throats below 1 micron.

## Estimation of Petrophysical Properties of Tar Sands Using Microtomography

- ID: SCA2010-21, year: 2010
- Authors: C. Caubit, A.P. Sheppard, Delottier L
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: micro-CT, tar sands, Lattice Boltzmann, formation factor, resistivity index, image segmentation
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2010/SCA2010-21.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2010-21)

Micro-CT images of tar-saturated unconsolidated sand samples were segmented and simulated to derive porosity, saturation, electrical properties and water relative permeability, with saturation overestimated by about 12 SU versus Dean Stark.

Conventional measurements on tar sands after bitumen extraction are suspect, so the authors imaged six tar-saturated unconsolidated samples (6 mm diameter, carefully punched and confined) at 5 micron resolution. Three-phase segmentation (grain, tar, void) showed tar mainly in the smaller pores. Porosity matched Dean Stark for the four fine, well-sorted samples but was overestimated by 2.3 to 4.1 PU for two coarse, poorly sorted samples. Segmented water saturation followed the Dean Stark trend but was about 12 SU too high, which accelerated solvent extraction confirmed as not a reference error. Simulated formation factors agreed with Humble's formula, resistivity index gave Archie n above 2 (about 2.9 to 5.1 best-fit), permeabilities were 3.4 to 10 D, and Lattice Boltzmann calculations on the water phase gave significant water relative permeability even at high tar saturation.

## Variation of wettability across different lithotypes in a reservoir and its impact on Digital Rock Physics pore scale simulations

- ID: SCA2024_1030, year: 2024
- Authors: Titly Farhana Faisal, Franck Nono, Mohamed Regaieg, Regis Brugidou, Florent Fischer, Cyril Caubit
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: digital rock physics, pore network modeling, mixed-wet wettability, relative permeability, wettability anchoring experiment, super-resolution micro-CT
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 5
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2024/SCA2024_1030.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2024_1030)

Wettability anchoring experiments on two sandstone lithotypes revealed mixed-wet small and large models; swapping wettability in pore network simulations changed Kr curves but barely affected recovery factors.

Fast DRP wettability anchoring experiments take three to four months, so the authors ask how often they are needed across a reservoir. Three sandstone lithotypes (LT1, LT2, LT3; permeability 4650, 1813 and 165 mD) from one well were imaged by micro-CT, super-resolved by a factor of 4 with ESRGAN, and converted to large pore networks of 1.5-2.4 million elements. Micro-CT Amott-like experiments on LT1 and LT3 showed mixed-wet small (MWS) wettability for LT1 and mixed-wet large (MWL) for LT3, with water saturation after spontaneous imbibition rising from 15% to 65% in LT1 and from 20% to 40% in LT3. About 1500 DynaPNM realizations per study were filtered by measured end-points and ranked P10/P50/P90. LT1 wettability applied to the LT2 network matched SCAL Kr and recovery on a sister sample. Interchanging wettabilities between LT1 and LT3 changed Kr shapes and Krw/Kro ratios, but recovery factors were barely affected and network topology mattered more.

## A Blind Study of Four Digital Rock Physics Vendor Labs on Porosity, Absolute Permeability, and Primary Drainage Capillary Pressure Data on Tight Outcrops

- ID: SCA2017-004, year: 2017
- Authors: Shreerang S. Chhatre, Hemant Sahoo, Sergio Leonardi, Keili Vidal
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: digital rock physics, blind study, tight rocks, primary drainage capillary pressure, centrifuge Pc, MICP
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 6
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2017/SCA2017-004.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2017-004)

Four commercial DRP labs blindly predicted porosity, permeability and drainage Pc on six tight outcrops; results diverged from lab measurements and each other, especially at high Pc.

Commercial digital rock physics (DRP) predictions are hard for users to judge against physical data. Six tight outcrops (3 carbonates, 3 sandstones, 0.01–1 mD) were measured for porosity and Klinkenberg permeability, then cut into quarters for four vendors, who predicted φ, k and primary drainage Pc using µCT only (phase I) and higher-resolution nano-CT/FIB-SEM (phase II). DRP porosity was 37–96% of measured values and permeability was off by factors of 0.4 to 4, with up to an order of magnitude scatter among vendors; the two tightest rocks could not be resolved by µCT. DRP Pc agreed with other vendors and centrifuge data at low Pc but diverged at high Pc, generally appearing more optimistic than the centrifuge bounds. The authors recommend validating DRP against experiments and requiring uncertainty quantification.

## Shale Reservoir Evaluation Improved by Dual-Energy X-Ray CT Imaging

- ID: SCA2013-044, year: 2013
- Authors: Joel Walls, Anyela Morcote, Juliana DeVito
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: dual-energy X-ray CT, bulk density and PEF, shale TOC, brittleness index, La Luna shale, digital rock physics
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2013/SCA2013-044.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2013-044)

Dual-energy CT of whole core from a La Luna shale well yields RHOB, PEF, TOC, mineralogy and brittleness logs within days, resembling Wolfcamp.

Traditional shale core analysis is slow, so the authors apply the first stage of Ingrain's digital rock workflow, CoreHD dual-energy X-ray CT at about 0.5 mm voxels, to the Tibu-176 well (La Luna Formation, Catatumbo Basin, Colombia). Continuous bulk density (RHOB) and photoelectric factor (PEF) logs were combined with spectral gamma data and XRF/XRD calibration to compute facies, mineralogy, TOC and a Rickman-type brittleness index. Results show a TOC sweet spot of about 7–9% by volume at 7870–7900 ft, with TOC elsewhere ranging from under 2% to over 10%. Mineralogy is mainly carbonate and silica with low clay, and brittleness index is generally above 80. Computed TOC agreed well with SEM-measured TOC, and RHOB-PEF data resemble the Wolfcamp more than the Eagle Ford. Data acquisition and interpretation took about 3–4 days.

## Three Dimensional Characterization of Matrix Acidizing by X-Ray Computed Tomography

- ID: SCA2014-046, year: 2014
- Authors: Henry Agbogun, Gabriela Giurea-Bica, Lazarus Ndatuwong, Ken Glover, Brent Nassichuk, Greg Henderson
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: matrix acidizing, wormhole, X-ray computed tomography, image registration, carbonate porosity change, acid stimulation
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2014/SCA2014-046.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2014-046)

Pre- and post-acid CT scans of four Swan Hills carbonate plugs are registered and subtracted to map 3-D wormholes and quantify porosity change of about 1.7-2.8%.

Wormhole characterization in matrix acidizing is usually one-dimensional, so the authors used medical CT to image wormholes in 3-D. Four 1.5-inch Swan Hills carbonate plugs (82-88% calcite, 10-15% dolomite) were scanned before and after acid flooding with HCl or reaction-safe acid at 85°C, 2500 psi confining and 500 psi backpressure. Post-treatment images were rigidly registered to pre-treatment images and subtracted, and the difference was calibrated to calcite grayscale to give porosity change. Each sample developed one dominant tortuous wormhole with branching. Wormhole tortuosity was 1.39-1.63 and porosity change 1.71-2.75%. Permeability rose from 0.02-2.52 mD to 54-1600 mD. Wormholes preferentially followed voxels with grayscale below the calcite mode of about 3200, indicating calcite-void mixtures.

## Fractal dimension of pore space in carbonate samples from Tushka Area (Egypt)

- ID: SCA2016-079, year: 2016
- Authors: Andreas Weller, Yi Ding, Zeyu Zhang, Mohamed Kassab, Matthias Halisch
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: fractal dimension, spectral induced polarization, mercury porosimetry, NMR T2 relaxation, effective hydraulic radius, carbonate pore size distribution
- Cited by 1 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2016/SCA2016-079.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2016-079)

Fractal dimensions of pore space in eight Egyptian limestones were derived from MP, NMR and SIP distributions and compared; SIP and NMR agree, surface dimension is lower.

Carbonate pore geometry is complex, which makes permeability prediction difficult, and fractal dimension from pore radius distributions offers an alternative. Eight Upper Cretaceous limestone plugs from the Tushka area (25 mm diameter, ~30 mm long) were measured with mercury porosimetry, NMR T2 (7.05 MHz) and spectral induced polarization (3 mHz–100 Hz). SIP relaxation time distributions from Debye decomposition were converted to pore radii assuming a Stern-layer model with a constant diffusion coefficient. Fractal dimension was taken as 3 minus the slope of log cumulative volume fraction versus log radius. SIP-derived dimensions ranged from 2.62 to 2.80 and were close to NMR values, with MP giving slightly larger ones, while the surface dimension from specific surface and effective hydraulic radius was lower (1.90–2.52). The MP median pore radius r50 agreed well with the effective hydraulic radius from permeability and formation factor.

## 1990 Sca Conference Paper Number 90001

- ID: SCA1990-01, year: 1990
- Authors: Petrographic Image Analysis, Robert Ehrlich, Edward L. Etris
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: petrographic image analysis, pore types, mercury injection capillary pressure, throat size, erosion-dilation, sample support
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1990/SCA1990-01.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1990-01)

Petrographic image analysis of thin sections yields 3-10 pore types per reservoir; regression against mercury injection links each type to a characteristic throat size, supporting permeability modelling.

The paper presents petrographic image analysis (PIA), which measures pore size and shape from thin-section porosity patches (porels) using erosion-dilation differencing to build smooth/rough spectra. An unmixing algorithm (SAWVECB) applied to a set of related samples, ten or more, derives 3-10 discrete pore types and their proportions in each sample. Multiple regression of mercury injection saturation increments on pore type proportions gives the throat-size distribution of each type, shown for the Benoist Sandstone with R2 of 0.78-0.95. Most reservoirs show a pore type/throat size association, strongest in unbioturbated, clay-poor rocks and persisting in recrystallized dolomites. The association is attributed to depositional domains of like pores, which also raises a sample support problem for small plugs. NMR T1 modes and imbibition/drainage curves are cited as independent support.

## 1990 Sca Conference Paper Number 90002

- ID: SCA1990-02, year: 1990
- Authors: Pore Image Characterization
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: petrographic image analysis, erosion-dilation, pore connectivity indicator, permeability prediction, pore size distribution, unmixing end members
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1990/SCA1990-02.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1990-02)

Erosion-dilation image analysis of thin sections gives pore size and a rough-area connectivity indicator that correlate with permeability in unconsolidated heavy oil sands from Alberta.

The paper uses petrographic image analysis to estimate permeability from small samples. An erosion-dilation algorithm gives a generalized pore size distribution without identifying individual pores, and the ratio of rough to total pore area serves as a 2-D connectivity indicator. The method was applied to 22 horizontal plugs from the Provost Upper Mannville B Pool, with permeability measured on 19. Vertical and horizontal thin sections (16 images each) were analyzed, and permeabilities from about 2 to 12 darcy were regressed on mean pore size and connectivity. Both regressions were significant at the 5% level, with vertical sections giving the better relationship because they sample horizontal laminae. Unmixing identified four pore end members tied to lithofacies, from large connected pores in blocky channel sands to small pores in channel margin sands.

## A Study of the Correlation Between Relative Permeability, Air Permeability and Depositional Environment on the Core-Plug Scale

- ID: SCA1991-10EURO, year: 1991
- Authors: Charlotte B. Tjølsen, Åse Scheie, Eivind Damsleth
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: relative permeability, air permeability regression, depositional environment, Corey exponent, irreducible water saturation, stochastic reservoir modelling
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1991/1-SCA1991-10EURO.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1991-10EURO)

Relative permeability measured on 85 plugs from one North Sea well is correlated with air permeability per depositional environment; only permeability, porosity and Swi regress well.

The study seeks relationships between air permeability and relative permeability for use in stochastic reservoir modelling. Unsteady-state relative permeability was measured at ambient conditions on 85 horizontal plugs from one well in a heterogeneous North Sea reservoir, after porous-plate establishment of Swi. Plugs were grouped into four depositional environments (distal and proximal mouthbar, distributary channel, tidal bay), and univariate statistics, correlation matrices and linear regressions on log10 Kair were computed. Each environment has distinct properties and correlation structure; proximal mouthbar has the highest mean permeability (2557 mD) and lowest Swi. Regression on log Kair fits permeability variables (R² 0.86–1.00) and Swi (0.48–0.82) well, but Corey exponents, Sor and krw endpoint fit poorly or inconsistently. The authors recommend sampling all environments and at least about 10 points per regression, with a wide permeability range.

## SCA1991-21EURO

- ID: SCA1991-21EURO, year: 1991
- Authors: Adler P.M, Jacquin C.G, Rahon D
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: reconstructed porous media, correlation function, Fontainebleau sandstone, permeability simulation, formation factor, thin section analysis
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1991/1-SCA1991-21EURO.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1991-21EURO)

3-D random pore structures are reconstructed from thin-section porosity and correlation function, then used to compute permeability and formation factor for Fontainebleau sandstone, matching trends within about a factor of 5.

The paper links 2-D thin-section geometry to 3-D transport properties of Fontainebleau sandstone. Porosity and the two-point correlation function were measured on binarized thin-section images, then used to generate 3-D discretized porous media (about 500,000 cells) with a Gaussian-field algorithm after Quiblier. Stokes flow and Laplace conduction were solved numerically on 65 reconstructed samples at porosities of 11, 14, 21 and 31%, and dynamic permeability was examined for a fracture and a two-length-scale geometry. Calculated permeability and formation factor reproduce the shape of the experimental porosity correlations (K ~ φ^4.15, F ~ φ^1.5) but differ from measurements by about a factor of 5. The authors attribute the discrepancy to the limited statistical description, discretization roughness, and finite-size effects. Some low-porosity realizations did not percolate, and at least 5 realizations per data set were needed.

## Extended Range Particle Size Distribution Using Laser Diffraction Technology: A New Perspective

- ID: SCA1991-26, year: 1991
- Authors: Ted J. Griffin Jr, Petrophysical Properties
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: laser diffraction, particle size distribution, grain size analysis, clay content XRD, sidewall core permeability, Vshale
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1991/1-SCA1991-26.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1991-26)

Laser diffraction gives rapid, reproducible particle size distributions of unconsolidated sands including silt and clay, agreeing with sieve, settling and XRD data and supporting Vshale and permeability estimation.

Traditional sieve, settling and thin-section grain size methods are slow and cannot classify silt and clay fractions. The paper adapts a Leeds & Northrup laser diffraction analyzer (5 mW, 750 nm laser, recirculating flow loop, about 30 s per measurement) to friable and unconsolidated Gulf Coast sands after disaggregation and sonication. Repeat and split-sample runs show good reproducibility. Results agree with dry sieving between 0.044 and 0.35 mm, with laser sizes exceeding sieve sizes above 0.35 mm because of elongate grains, and with a 20-minute settling test. Laser clay fractions compare favorably with XRD clay content for three Gulf Coast sample sets. The full distribution allows Folk and moment sorting parameters and Vshale to be computed, and a texture coefficient correlates with plug permeability (log k = 0.059·TC − 1.03, r = 0.955) for sidewall core permeability estimation.

## Surface Profilometry and Petrophysical Measurements

- ID: SCA1992-27, year: 1992
- Authors: M. T. Myers, R. DiFoggio
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: surface profilometry, power spectrum, cross-over frequency, grain size, permeability prediction, fractal dimension
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1992/1-SCA1992-27.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1992-27)

Stylus profilometry and power spectra of sandpaper and ceramic surfaces yield grain size, permeability and porosity estimates; the cross-over frequency tracks grain size and permeability.

The paper tests whether surface profiles can predict petrophysical properties, using sandpapers (220-600 grit) and four commercial ceramics (porosity 0.33-0.45, air permeability 1.1-74) as artificial samples. A diamond-stylus profilometer made 3 mm scans (5 µm tip, 512 points), with six scans averaged per surface. Roughness statistics (Rq, Ra, Ry) decrease with finer grit. Power spectra show a low-frequency slope and a steeper high-frequency slope, with a cross-over frequency that matches sieve mesh size for sandpaper. For the ceramics, permeability scales with the square of the cross-over frequency, and the low-frequency slope correlates with porosity (r = 0.96). High-frequency slopes exceed three, so the surfaces are not fractal at grain scale. Real rocks have yet to be fully tested.

## Pore Geometry and Rock Properties

- ID: SCA1995-21, year: 1995
- Authors: Peter R Whattler, Paul B. Basan, Applied Reservoir, Brian P. Moss, Moss Petrophysics
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: backscattered electron imaging, image analysis porosity, permeability prediction, formation resistivity factor, pore geometry, effective porosity
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1995/SCA1995-21.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1995-21)

Backscattered electron image porosity from 82 North Sea sandstone samples predicts permeability far better than core porosity and gives a reciprocal estimator for formation factor.

The study compares special core analysis data with pore parameters from backscattered electron image analysis on 82 Tertiary submarine-fan sandstone samples from nine North Sea wells. Image porosity, which captures mainly pores larger than about 3 µm, underestimates gas-expansion porosity by 10-15 porosity units and does not correlate with it. However, image porosity predicts permeability with R² = 0.855 and σ = 0.284 log10 units, versus σ = 0.637 for core porosity. Adding a pore length-squared term improves this to σ = 0.266 (R² = 0.875). Formation factor is approximated by 4.3 + 1/image porosity for about 80% of the data (Trend A), while well E is an outlier and was excluded from the regressions. The authors conclude that image porosity approximates effective, flowing porosity rather than total porosity.

## Relationships Between Porosity and Permeability for Porous Rocks

- ID: SCA1996-10, year: 1996
- Authors: Shouxiang Ma, Norman R. Morrow
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: porosity-permeability correlation, Thomeer model, mercury injection capillary pressure, Swanson parameter, threshold pressure, pore structure factor
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1996/SCA1996-10.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1996-10)

Tests porosity-permeability correlations on 578 samples with mercury injection data; the Swanson parameter predicts permeability best (R=0.95), far above porosity alone (R=0.42).

Porosity-permeability correlations are weak and depend on pore structure. The authors analyzed 578 sandstone and carbonate samples with mercury injection capillary pressure curves, fitting 463 of them with the Thomeer model (R≥0.85) to obtain threshold pressure PT and pore structure factor G. Log permeability versus porosity gave R=0.42, but subdividing by G or PT improved the correlations markedly. Permeability correlated with PT at R=0.82, with the Katz-Thompson φ/PT form at 0.87, the Thomeer parameter at 0.93, and the Swanson parameter at 0.95 (k = 376(φSN/Pc)max^1.54). Simple pore-throat models show that identical porosity can give very different permeability, and a sample-size criterion is proposed for capillary pressure measurements on cuttings.

## Cathodoluminescence and trace element distribution in authigenic quartz in sandstones

- ID: SCA1996-13, year: 1996
- Authors: Maurice PAGEL, Christelle DEMARS, Etienne DELOULE, Philippe BLANC, Jocelyn BARBARAND
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: cathodoluminescence, authigenic quartz overgrowth, SIMS trace elements, aluminium content, sandstone diagenesis
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1996/SCA1996-13.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1996-13)

Combines cathodoluminescence spectra with SIMS and microprobe trace element analysis of quartz overgrowths, finding the 330-340 nm CL band correlates with Al content, enhanced by alpha irradiation.

The causes of cathodoluminescence (CL) variations in authigenic quartz are only partly understood, which limits their use to interpret diagenetic conditions. Quartz overgrowths on detrital grains in sandstones were studied with CL spectroscopy (200-900 nm), secondary ion mass spectrometry for Al, Li, Na, K, Ti and Mn in the ppm range, and electron microprobe for Al. Trace element content varies within an overgrowth, between overgrowths in one sample and between formations. Al reaches up to 3000 ppm in Triassic sandstones of the Paris basin and the Ardeche paleomargin, but is generally below 400 ppm in some oil field reservoirs, and Li increases with Al. The intensity of the 330-340 nm CL band correlates with Al content, and alpha irradiation at the Oklo uranium deposit (Al up to 1400 ppm) increases it. The cause of the emission is still not definitively established, and synthetic doped crystals are needed.

## New insights on diagenetic quartz overgrowths by cathodoluminescence

- ID: SCA1996-14, year: 1996
- Authors: Benoît Maréchal, Alain Chartier, Philippe Blanc, Médard Thiry, Nicole Guilhaumou, Claudine Durand
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: cathodoluminescence, quartz overgrowths, SEM image analysis, Fontainebleau sandstone, CL spectra, diagenesis
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1996/SCA1996-14.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1996-14)

SEM cathodoluminescence and image analysis quantify quartz cement in Fontainebleau sandstone, while spectra of North Sea overgrowths show no distinct impurity signature, suggesting zoning reflects growth kinetics.

Quartz overgrowths reduce reservoir porosity but are hard to quantify petrographically. SEM cathodoluminescence (CL) images combined with secondary-electron images were segmented to separate detrital grains, cement and pores in Fontainebleau quartzite. Cement reached about 35% with isopachous silica layers (residual porosity 1-2%), versus about 30% and 5% porosity for subeuhedral overgrowths; initial porosity was about 35%. CL spectra of Brent Group (Dunbar, North Sea) detrital grains and overgrowth zones showed bands at the same wavelengths, with only intensity differing. Prolonged irradiation produced new bands at 286, 450 and 640 nm in all grains, so zonation is attributed to growth rate or recrystallization rather than incorporated elements. Quantification remains time consuming, needing about 40 views per thin section.

## Petrographic Image Analysis for Prediction of Petrophysical Properties and Waterflood Displacement Efficiency: Some Missing Links

- ID: SCA1997-20, year: 1997
- Authors: N. C. Wardlaw, S. B. Coskun
- Method: review or workflow
- Keywords: petrographic image analysis, pore types, permeability prediction, initial water saturation, residual oil saturation, pore-throat size correlation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
