- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1997/SCA1997-20.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1997-20)

Reviews petrographic image analysis of 2-d thin sections for predicting porosity, permeability, capillary pressure, Swi and Sor, showing pore size and shape govern these properties.

The paper reviews Petrographic Image Analysis (PIA), which quantifies 2-d pore geometry in epoxy-impregnated thin sections using erosion-dilation and Q-mode factor analysis to define pore types, then regresses them against core-test properties. Although throats cannot be seen in 2-d, permeability and drainage capillary pressure can be predicted, attributed to the correlation between pore and throat sizes. An empirical permeability model using porosity and the largest inscribed circle diameters gave R² of 0.90. For two sandstone reservoirs (North American and North Sea), pore-type models explained 72% and 73% of Swi variation and 64% and 79% of Sor variation. Swi rises with the volume of small pores, while Sor increases with pore non-uniformity and decreasing connectivity. PIA requires a training set of tested samples and supplements rather than replaces core testing.

## Core Sample Permeability Estimation Using Statistical Image Analysis

- ID: SCA1997-23, year: 1997
- Authors: Ioannis Chatzis, Demck M. Jewlal, Marios A. Ioannidis
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: image analysis, autocorrelation function, permeability prediction, two-point correlation, back-scatter SEM, Monte Carlo
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1997/SCA1997-23.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1997-23)

Permeability is predicted from 2-D BSE image porosity and autocorrelation integral scale via a power-law model; about 10-40 images per sample suffice.

The paper develops a phenomenological permeability model, k = A<phi>^B<I>^C, using average image porosity and the integral scale of the autocorrelation function from 2-D back-scatter SEM images. It was calibrated on 14 core samples from four Western Canadian formations (about 60 images each, permeability spanning three orders of magnitude). Image porosity and ACF integral scale were found to be independent, supporting the two-parameter form. The model performed better than the Berryman-Blair Kozeny-Carman approach, which suffers from magnification-dependent specific surface area, and predicted permeabilities of Coskun and Wardlaw's sandstones well. Monte Carlo trials showed the effective permeability estimate is least sensitive to image count, with little gain beyond about 40 images.

## Prediction of Electrical and Flow Properties of Reservoir Rocks of Varying Wettability Characteristics

- ID: SCA1997-26, year: 1997
- Authors: J.L. Wang, A. Gupta, Geol. Eng
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network model, resistivity index, wettability, corner and film flow, effective medium theory, disjoining pressure
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1997/SCA1997-26.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1997-26)

A pore-network model with triangular throats, corner and film flow predicts resistivity index and relative permeability for water-, mixed- and oil-wet rocks, and is most sensitive to coordination number.

The paper presents a network model of spherical pore bodies and triangular pore throats that includes corner and thin-film transport, to predict resistivity index and relative permeability as a function of wettability. Effective medium theory and Bethe-tree accessibility give network conductances, and pore size distributions come from mercury injection data. Film thickness follows a measured disjoining pressure isotherm. For sandstone sample S-28, parameters were optimized against measured porosity, permeability and formation factor (0.297, 5200 md, 9.17), giving predicted values of 0.298, 5080 md and 9.13. Sensitivity analysis shows the strongest influence from coordination number, then shape factor and the throat length parameters C and alpha. Water in corners and films strongly affects resistivity index but has little effect on relative permeability, and the relative permeability predictions need further improvement.

## A New Look at Analyzing Petrographic Data: The Fuzzy Logic Approach

- ID: SCA1997-31, year: 1997
- Authors: Adwait Chawathé, Maqsood Ali, Ahmed Ouenes, William Weiss
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: fuzzy logic, minipermeameter, thin section petrography, permeability controls, Shattuck sandstone, Santa Rosa sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1997/SCA1997-31.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1997-31)

Combines minipermeameter grids with subdivided thin sections and a fuzzy logic algorithm to rank petrographic controls on permeability in two sandstones, agreeing largely with regression.

Conventional regression ranking of petrographic controls on permeability compares different curve fits unevenly and relies on petrographer judgment. The authors propose measuring permeability on a fine minipermeameter grid and dividing each thin section into ~10 mm areas matching the probe's investigation volume, yielding data equivalent to 6 to 14 thin sections per section. A fuzzy logic algorithm (Lin, 1994) using fuzzy curves ranks inputs by their fuzzy range. It was applied to Upper Queen Shattuck sandstone (267 data points, 38 thin sections, 16 elements) and Santa Rosa sandstone (150 points, 12 thin sections). Porosity types, especially secondary intergranular porosity, dominate permeability in both, and fuzzy ranking agrees well with regression. Discrepancies arose from skewed data (a single microporosity point raised its fuzzy range from ~207 to 372) and from cross-correlated variables such as quartz.

## Computer-Enhanced Core Analysis

- ID: SCA1997-37, year: 1997
- Authors: M.A. Ioannidis, I. Chatzis, M.J. Kwiecien
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: stochastic pore structure reconstruction, network modeling, SEM image analysis, autocorrelation function, coordination number, resistivity index
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1997/SCA1997-37.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1997-37)

Combines SEM image statistics, 3-D stochastic reconstruction and network modeling to predict permeability, formation factor and resistivity index; low-permeability samples are over-predicted owing to resolution and homogeneity limits.

The paper presents a workflow to predict petrophysical properties from 2-D back-scatter SEM images of thin sections. Porosity and autocorrelation are used to build 3-D stochastic reconstructions (256^3 voxels) of 14 sandstone and dolomite samples. The reconstructions are characterized for pore and throat size distributions and coordination number (about four when two-coordinated pores are excluded), and the results feed network models. Resistivity index versus saturation for sample 58A agreed well with experiment without adjustable parameters, but permeability was consistently over-predicted for samples below 10 mD. Doubling the reconstruction resolution for sample 30B lowered predicted permeability to 6.2 mD, which shows resolution sensitivity. Stationarity assumptions give pore and throat size distributions that are too narrow.

## Specific Internal Surface :the "Forgotten?"Petrophysical Measurement! or Application of Fractal Pore Models for Improved Permeability Estimations from Logs!

- ID: SCA1998-40, year: 1998
- Authors: Lutz Riepe
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: specific internal surface, fractal pore model, Kozeny-Carman equation, BET surface area, NMR permeability prediction, PARIS equation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1998/SCA1998-40.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1998-40)

Advocates reviving the Clausthal Concept, using fractal specific internal surface in modified Kozeny/Carman equations (PARIS, Budapest, Aberdeen) to predict permeability from logs, mainly NMR.

Kozeny/Carman permeability equations fail when a measured BET or image-analysis surface is inserted directly, because the measured surface depends on resolution. The Clausthal Concept corrects this with surface magnification factors from a self-similar "pigeon hole" fractal pore model, giving the PARIS equation K = 475.3/F (Spor,BET/q0)^-3.1085. Substituting NMR relaxation (relaxivity 0.66 s/mm for sandstones) gives the Budapest equation, and substituting thorium concentration gives the Aberdeen equation. Lab-derived permeabilities matched measured values within one order of magnitude over 0.1 mD to 10 D. Field use of the thorium and electrical versions was limited by tool resolution, geochemical scatter and high salinity, so modern NMR logging, calibrated on cores, offers the best prospect.

## Porosity and Permeability of Palaeocene Sands from the North Sea; a Comparison of Plug Porosity and Image Derived Porosity

- ID: SCA1998-46, year: 1998
- Authors: Kate L. Vigar, Mike Lovell, Peter K. Harvey.
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: image porosity, back-scattered SEM, plug porosity, gas permeability, clay content, Palaeocene sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1998/SCA1998-46.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1998-46)

Back-scattered SEM image porosity correlates better with permeability than helium plug porosity in North Sea Palaeocene sands, and clay content largely explains the plug porosity-permeability scatter.

The porosity-permeability relationship in a Palaeocene sand reservoir is very poor, so image analysis was used to explain it. Back-scattered SEM images at x30 and x150 from endtrims of 48 plugs gave a 2D image porosity, which was compared with helium plug porosity and gas permeability. Image porosity correlated better with permeability (r = 0.73) than plug porosity (r = 0.41) and gave lower values. Pore-size splitting showed low permeability samples have many small pores and high permeability samples have large pores. Pore anisotropy, tested on cubic plugs, was not significant. Plug porosity tracked the area of pores plus clay, and subtracting one third of the clay area improved the match, so clay is a major control on the plug porosity-permeability relationship.

## A Systematic Study for Selecting an Adequate Tortuosity Model

- ID: SCA1999-49, year: 1999
- Authors: Ali. A. Garrouch, Liaqat Ali, Fuad Qasem, Abdullah S. Ebrahim
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: tortuosity, effective diffusion coefficient, formation factor, four-electrode resistivity, capillary-tube permeability model, pore throat distribution
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1999/SCA1999-49.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1999-49)

Tortuosity from helium-nitrogen diffusion and electrical formation factor was compared for 14 sandstones; Brakel-Heertjes and Pirson models agreed best and enabled permeability prediction.

Many tortuosity models exist for diffusion and electrical conductivity, and it is unclear which suits a given rock. Fourteen sandstone plugs (Berea, Okesa, Tallant, Elgin; mean permeability about 1024 md, porosity about 24%) were measured for counter-current He-N2 effective diffusion, four-electrode formation factor, and centrifuge air-brine capillary pressure. Effective diffusion coefficients were obtained by matching Crank-Nicolson solutions of the diffusion equation to concentration data. Tortuosity from analogous diffusion and electrical models was compared; most values fell between 2 and 3, and Brakel-Heertjes versus Pirson differed least (8.5 ± 4.9%). A capillary-tube permeability model using this tortuosity and a pore geometric factor h = 10·a_a/τ² (a_a being the area under the pore-throat distribution function) reproduced measured permeability with 6.72% average absolute error.

## Prediction of Petrophysical Parameters in Sandstones by Use of SEM/Image Analysis

- ID: SCA1999-50, year: 1999
- Authors: Reidar Bøe, Svein Mjaaland, Erik Lieng, Torleif Holt, Dag Wessel-Berg.
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: SEM image analysis, backscattered electron imaging, pore size distribution, Kozeny-Carman permeability, capillary pressure prediction, pore surface mineralogy
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1999/SCA1999-50.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1999-50)

SEM/BSE image analysis of sandstone thin sections predicts porosity, permeability, irreducible saturation and capillary pressure curves compared with plug data, and links surface mineralogy to wettability.

The study derives petrophysical parameters from thin sections of Jurassic sandstones (Frøy, Rind, Mid-Norwegian shelf) using mosaics of 16 BSE images plus X-ray element maps. Image porosity is lower than plug He-porosity but matches it after adding 55% of the clay content as microporosity (75 samples). Permeability correlates with macroporosity (R²=0.79), median pore size (0.89) and specific pore surface (0.90); multiple regression gives R²=0.92 and a modified Kozeny–Carman equation about 0.89–0.90. Capillary pressure curves from a capillary-bundle model with estimated throat radii preserve the ranking of porous-plate curves, and irreducible saturation at 12 bar fits best with 40% of clay as microporosity (R²=0.79, 12 samples). Amott tests on two clay-rich sandstones and Berea with oils containing acid or base additives show wettability depends on additive type and surface mineralogy, suggesting a mineralogy-based wettability prediction approach.

## Multi-Scale Flow Zone Determinations

- ID: SCA1999-59, year: 1999
- Authors: James J. Funk, Shameem Siddiqui, Abdulrazak Al-Sardi, Mohammad Amin
- Method: field or reservoir case study
- Keywords: flow zone indicator, reservoir quality index, Carman-Kozeny, Lorenz coefficient, X-ray CT, scale dependence
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/1999/SCA1999-59.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA1999-59)

Flow zone indicators from CT, plug, whole core and flow meter data in an Upper Jurassic carbonate show plug RQI best matches well inflow; whole core averages out permeability contrasts.

The paper tests the Carman-Kozeny hydraulic radius flow zone method (RQI, NPI, FZI) across scales in a heterogeneous Upper Jurassic carbonate. Data came from open-hole flow meter results, 2-5/8 inch whole core (about 200 samples), 1.5 inch plugs at 0.5 ft spacing (about 350 samples), and X-ray CT slices converted to porosity and permeability. Cumulative plug RQI gave the best match to the flow meter profile, while whole core data followed the general trend but missed permeability layering near X880 ft. Using FZI instead of RQI degraded the match, consistent with Nelson's pore-volume-based specific surface argument. Whole core Lorenz coefficients were about half those of plug data, reflecting permeability averaging, and CT distributions showed the difficulty of obtaining representative samples even within one foot.

## A Pore Space Inversion to Enhance Reservoir Characterization in a Porous Diagenetic Carbonate

- ID: SCA2001-32, year: 2001
- Authors: G. N. Boitnott, New England, J. J. Funk
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: pore structure inversion, carbonate, NMR T2, mercury injection, ultrasonic velocity, throat shape
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-32.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-32)

A pore structure inversion constrained by acoustic, flow and electrical data on eight carbonate plugs yields bimodal throat models that match NMR and mercury injection.

Eight plugs from a fine-grained diagenetic carbonate were measured for ultrasonic velocity, permeability and resistivity versus effective pressure, with NMR T2 and mercury injection as additional data. Optical microscopy could not resolve the submicron pore network, so a pore structure inversion (PSI) was applied using porosity, permeability, resistivity and dynamic bulk modulus as constraints. Length scale, nodal fraction and coordination number were explored; short length scales (about 3.3 microns) fit best, and adding surface-area-to-volume as a constraint corrected T2 peak times that were otherwise too high. About a third of porosity is nodal, and throats are bimodal: tubular throats with radii just under 1 micron and crack-like throats of 3 to 5 microns. Forward predictions of NMR and mercury injection agreed closely with measurements. The compliant crack-like throats explain velocity scatter and short T2 times, yet stay hidden from mercury injection and transport properties, which explains why T2 poorly predicts permeability.

## Effect of Pore Size Distribution on Porosity Measurement by Computurized Tomography

- ID: SCA2001-49, year: 2001
- Authors: DEMIR Murat, Middle East
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: X-ray CT porosity, air CT number, pore size distribution, single scan method, beam hardening, Hounsfield unit
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-49.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-49)

Air CT number varies with pore size rather than being fixed at -1000; a pore-size-corrected single-scan CT method gives porosities about 5% lower (relative) than the classic method.

CT porosity calculations assume an air CT number of -1000 HU, but earlier work at METU showed deviations from this value. The authors scanned a synthetic plexiglass core of 19 slices with artificial pores of 1.5 to 15 mm in a water bath at 100 and 130 keV. Air CT number approached -1000 as pore size increased, and data from pores below about 4-4.5 mm were excluded from curve fitting because of high scatter. The resulting correlations were used in a new single-scan method that sums partial air CT numbers weighted by partial porosity from mercury capillary pressure or thin-section pore size distributions. On Berea, two limestones and a dolomite, the new method gave porosities closer to helium porosimetry (Berea 20.01-20.37% versus 20.57% by helium and 21.02% by classic CT). Results were about 5% lower in relative terms than classic single-scan CT.

## Hydraulic Units Approach Conditioned by Well Testing for Better Permeability Modelling in a North Africa Oil Field

- ID: SCA2001-50, year: 2001
- Authors: Yasin Ellabad, Patrick Corbett, Richard Straub
- Method: field or reservoir case study
- Keywords: hydraulic units, flow zone indicator, Lorenz plot, stochastic simulation, well test validation, permeability modelling
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-50.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-50)

Hydraulic units from core data are modelled stochastically with three correlation lengths, validated against production logging and well tests, giving a good history match in a Triassic fluvial reservoir.

Conventional log-permeability versus porosity regression is a poor basis for permeability modelling in heterogeneous rock, so the authors use Flow Zone Indicator-based hydraulic units (HUs) from core plug data in a Triassic fluvial reservoir in North Africa. The HUs are classified on a poro-perm plot, and a Lorenz plot shows that HU1 supplies about 70% of the flow but only 20% of the storativity. Production logging inflow profiles over the perforated intervals confirm that HU1 (coarse-grained material) controls inflow. HUs were distributed with a pixel-based stochastic model in three versions (homogeneous, heterogeneous, very heterogeneous) that differ only in spatial continuity. Synthetic well tests were compared with actual well test data, and the chosen model was transferred to flow simulation with HU-specific saturation, capillary pressure, relative permeability and PVT data. Permeability needed only slight tuning to obtain a good history match. The authors conclude that fine-scale HU definition at the wellbore should be preserved during scale-up.

## Micro-Scale Two-Phase Flow Simulation of Lattice Boltzman Method for Capillary Hysteresis

- ID: SCA2001-55, year: 2001
- Authors: Hiroshi Mitsuishi, Hiroshi Okabe
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: lattice Boltzmann method, capillary hysteresis, snap-off, film flow, wettability, pore-scale simulation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2001/SCA2001-55.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2001-55)

2D lattice Boltzmann simulations reproduce snap-off, film flow and capillary hysteresis in simple pore models of Berea sandstone, with wettability-dependent behavior.

Relative permeability and capillary pressure cannot be uniquely determined from core flooding unless one is known, so the authors explore the Lattice Boltzmann Method (LBM) as a pore-scale two-phase flow simulator. Preliminary 2D simulations used simple pore models built from the throat size distribution of Berea sandstone, and a virtual core was used to vary pore shape and wettability. The simulations reproduced snap-off and oil film flow on oil-wet walls. Snap-off did not occur at small aspect ratio (body-to-throat size) or low interfacial tension. Simulated drainage capillary pressure was nearly equal to that estimated from throat width and surface tension. Imbibition began below a critical pressure set by surface tension and throat width, and hysteresis arose from the two-dimensional pore shape. Residual oil shape and recovery rate differed among water-wet, neutral and oil-wet conditions, though the findings are qualitative.

## The Missing Link Between Pore-Scale Anchoring and Pore-Scale Prediction

- ID: SCA2002-25, year: 2002
- Authors: Steven R. McDougall, Ken S. Sorbie
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: pore network model, percolation theory, mercury injection capillary pressure, coordination number, gas-oil relative permeability, residual saturation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2002/SCA2002-25.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2002-25)

Percolation theory combined with MICP radii and residual saturations gives unique network parameters (z, n, v), enabling gas-oil relative permeability prediction, verified on blind simulations and one clastic core.

Anchoring pore network models to MICP data alone leaves an infinite set of parameter combinations, so relative permeability cannot be predicted uniquely. The authors use percolation theory (z·Pth = d/(d-1)) with the percolation radius Rp, the wetting-phase trapping radius Rt, Rmin and Rmax to obtain two equations that uniquely fix the coordination number z and pore-size exponent n. Residual wetting and trapped nonwetting saturations then give the volume exponent v. Tested on 21 blind 3D network simulations, average errors were 2.14% for z and 2.21% for n; v from Swr or Snwt alone had about 19% error, but their average gave 3.32%. Predicted gas-oil relative permeabilities matched the blind simulations and one clastic core (conductivity exponent 3.8). Rp and Rt currently require flow data, and future work aims to infer them from capillary pressure curves alone.

## Multiple-Point Statistics to Generate Geologically Realistic Pore-Space Representations

- ID: SCA2003-09, year: 2003
- Authors: Martin J. Blunt
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: multiple-point statistics, pore-space reconstruction, Fontainebleau sandstone, thin-section training image, percolation connectivity, micro-CT
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-09.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-09)

Multiple-point statistics from a 2D thin-section training image generate 3D Fontainebleau sandstone pore-space models that preserve long-range connectivity better than two-point statistical methods.

Two-point statistical reconstructions of 3D pore space from thin sections often fail to reproduce long-range connectivity. The authors adapt geostatistical multiple-point statistics, using a binarized 300×300 pixel (7.5 µm) thin-section slice as the training image with a multigrid of four 9×9 templates, and rotate the 2D statistics to build an isotropic 200³ 3D model. They test it on Fontainebleau sandstone with a micro-CT reference (porosity 0.1355). The reconstruction took 37 hours of CPU time. Specific surface area was 0.146 versus 0.122 for micro-CT, and 8% of pore space was disconnected versus 2% in the reference. The fraction of percolating cells was much closer to micro-CT than Gaussian-field or simulated-annealing reconstructions, though still below process-based reconstruction.

## A Method for Estimating Permeability of Carbonate Rocks from CT Scan Data

- ID: SCA2003-41, year: 2003
- Authors: Ebrahim Hassanzadeh, S. Saleh Hendi, Ayato Kato
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: CT scan, permeability prediction, artificial neural network, carbonate Sarvak Formation, PDPK pressure decay permeametry, CT number upscaling
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-41.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-41)

A back-propagation neural network trained on CT slice averages, porosity, density and mineralogy predicts carbonate permeability of held-out Sarvak samples better than multiple linear regression.

Estimating carbonate permeability is difficult because pore types vary at the same porosity. The authors used 34 plugs (1.5 in x 2.5 in) from the Sarvak Formation in four Iranian wells, measuring porosity, PDPK pressure-decay permeability, mineral percentages from thin sections, and CT slices (120 keV, 2 mm spacing, 32 slices per sample). Slice-average CT numbers, porosity, depth, bulk density and mineralogy were used as inputs to a feed-forward back-propagation network with permeability as target, with 22 training and 12 test samples. Single-variable crossplots showed little correlation with permeability, and multiple linear regression gave only moderate correlation. The network followed measured permeability (0.05 to 179 mD) closely in test samples, with accuracy improving as more CT averages were retained, especially for low permeability. The authors note that results depend on adequate training data and are lab specific.

## Core Reconstruction from CT Scan Porosity Fields

- ID: SCA2003-48, year: 2003
- Authors: A. Moctezuma-Berthier, O. Vizika, P.M. Adler
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: CT scan porosity map, stochastic reconstruction, Gaussian correlated field, box integration method, carbonate permeability, electrical conductivity
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-48.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-48)

Stochastic reconstruction of carbonate cores from CT porosity maps works well for Brauvilliers but poorly for Estaillades; conductivity and permeability are computed and compared with measurements.

The paper reconstructs 3D porosity fields of cores from CT-scan porosity maps. Long porosity trends are removed, the porosities are transformed to a Gaussian variable, and a correlated Gaussian field is generated by a Fourier technique and then back-transformed. Applied to Brauvilliers and Estaillades carbonates, the reconstruction reproduced the correlation function and visual appearance for Brauvilliers (isotropic), while for the orthotropic Estaillades it reproduced the anisotropy but not the experimental correlations. Conductivity and permeability were computed by box integration using local porosity correlations. Conductivity agreed well with measurements for Brauvilliers, while the Estaillades measurement was above the master curve. Permeability for Brauvilliers (measured 115 mD) was insensitive to the local K(e) correlation, whereas Estaillades (219 mD) showed a significant influence.

## A Structural Model to Predict Transport Properties of Granular Porous Media

- ID: SCA2003-53, year: 2003
- Authors: Guy Chauveteau, Yuchun Kuang, Marc Fleury
- Method: theory or analytical model
- Keywords: Grain and Pore Throat model, permeability, formation factor, critical porosity, Forchheimer inertia, colloid deposition
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-53.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-53)

The Grain and Pore Throat model links permeability, formation factor, resistivity index and other transport properties to pore-throat geometry through power laws of (porosity minus critical porosity), agreeing with available data.

Empirical Archie and Kozeny-Carman relations lack physical basis for transport exponents, and the sphere-in-cell and capillary-bundle models give unrealistic velocity fields. The Grain and Pore Throat (GPT) model assumes pressure drop is localized in pore throats, so permeability depends on throat diameter, throat length and unit cell size. Transport properties are predicted to scale as (porosity − critical porosity)^a, with a derived from geometry. Permeability of Fontainebleau sandstones fits an exponent of 3.98 against the predicted 4 with a residual porosity of 2%, and the formation factor exponent of −1.5 corresponds to a throat aspect ratio near 2. The model also explains stress-induced permeability changes in Berea sandstone, Forchheimer inertial coefficient trends, polymer shear rates, and colloid deposition simulated with PARIS without fitting parameters.

## Enhanced Modelling of Mercury Porosimetry, Absolute Permeability and Formation Damage Using a Three-Dimensional Pore-Network Model

- ID: SCA2003-56, year: 2003
- Authors: G.Peter Matthews
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: mercury intrusion porosimetry, pore network model, formation damage, Boltzmann-annealed Simplex, pore shielding, colloid occlusion
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2003/SCA2003-56.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2003-56)

A Pore-Cor 3D pore network, fitted to mercury intrusion curves with a Boltzmann-annealed Simplex, predicts formation damage from colloid occlusion that starts later and falls faster than capillary bundle models.

The paper presents the Pore-Cor network model, which builds 3D unit cells of up to 1000 cuboidal pores joined by throats, to extract more information from mercury intrusion porosimetry. The network is fitted to the measured intrusion curve with a Boltzmann-annealed Simplex over four parameters (throat skew, pore skew, connectivity, correlation level) plus Boolean constraints, and it accounts for shielding of large pores by small throats. It was applied to a poorly consolidated turbidite sandstone (gas permeability 3335 mD, porosity 33.4%, modelled porosity 27.89%), with ten stochastic realizations giving close fits. Simulated colloid occlusion shows permeability loss beginning at larger included particle sizes (about 8 mm particle) than the capillary bundle model, then dropping more sharply to zero at 15-18 mm. Visualizations show small occluded throats blocking access to large pores.

## Predicting Permeability Through 3D Pore-Space Images Reconstructed Using Multiple-Point Statistics

- ID: SCA2004-24, year: 2004
- Authors: M.J. Blunt
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: multiple-point statistics, pore-space reconstruction, lattice Boltzmann permeability, thin section training image, carbonate, effective medium approximation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2004/SCA2004-24.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2004-24)

3D pore-space images of a carbonate are reconstructed from a 2D thin section using multiple-point statistics; lattice Boltzmann permeability overestimates the measured value within a factor of three.

Micro-CT cannot resolve the sub-micron pores of many carbonates, and two-point statistics underestimate pore connectivity. The authors reconstruct 3D pore structures from a 512×512 binary thin section (0.345 µm/pixel) of a Middle East bioclastic packstone/grainstone using multiple-point statistics, with a 9×9 template and four-level multigrid. Permeability of the reconstructions, averaged over five realizations, was computed with a D3Q19 lattice-Boltzmann model and compared with a 2D effective medium approximation (EMA). The measured permeability is 6.7 md at porosity 0.318; EMA gave 6.05 md and LBM gave 19.8 md, an overestimate within a factor of three. Berea sandstone was used for comparison (1100 md measured, 1448 md EMA, 1274 md LBM). The authors attribute the carbonate overestimate to the small training image and suggest larger templates and multiple thin sections.

## Modeling Pore Geometric and Wettability Trends of Multiphase Flow in Porous Media

- ID: SCA2004-26, year: 2004
- Authors: P.H. Valvatne, X.D. Jing, R.M.M. Smits
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network modeling, relative permeability, wettability, mercury injection capillary pressure, NMR T2, Amott index
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2004/SCA2004-26.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2004-26)

A Berea-derived pore network is tuned to capillary pressure and NMR data to predict relative permeability across water-wet, mixed-wet and oil-wet sandstones, and to explore geometric and wettability trends.

Predictive pore-scale modeling normally needs a network from imaging of the specific rock. Here a reference Berea network (12,349 pores, 26,146 throats) keeps its topology while throat radii are adjusted to match mercury or centrifuge capillary pressure and pore volumes are constrained by NMR T2. Sensitivity studies show that a wider throat size distribution (higher λ) lowers oil relative permeability and, in water-wet cases, raises residual oil saturation from 0.3 to 0.6 as λ goes from 0.2 to 1.4. In mixed-wet cases residual oil is 0.07-0.20, and an oil-wet fraction near 0.4 marks the percolation threshold that sharply reduces residual oil. Without further adjustment, the model predicted relative permeability for water-wet Berea, a mixed-wet sandstone (Amott water index 0.56 predicted vs 0.55 measured) and an oil-wet sandstone (Amott indices 0.00 and 0.15 vs 0.00 and 0.14) in good agreement with data.

## Petrotyping: A Basemap and Atlas for Navigating Through Permeability and Porosity Data for Reservoir Comparison and Permeability Prediction

- ID: SCA2004-30, year: 2004
- Authors: P.W.M. Corbett, D.K. Potter
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: flow zone indicator, hydraulic units, global hydraulic elements, petrotype, porosity-permeability, permeability prediction
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2004/SCA2004-30.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2004-30)

Proposes ten predefined global hydraulic elements based on FZI boundaries as a universal porosity-permeability basemap for comparing reservoirs and predicting permeability.

Conventional hydraulic unit analysis gives different units for each well, making comparison between wells and fields difficult. The authors define ten global hydraulic elements (GHE1-10) with fixed FZI lower boundaries doubling from 0.0938 to 48, forming a template on a porosity-permeability crossplot. Plug data from shallow marine reservoirs (Siberia, North Sea, North Africa) plot as clear progressions across GHEs that follow coarsening-upward grain size. Chalk falls in a single GHE (GHE1, uni-petrotype), whereas a Middle East carbonate spans several GHEs (multi-petrotype). The approach is compared with Winland R35 typing and is proposed for reservoir comparison, selecting training data and permeability prediction.

## Estimating the Permeability of Carbonate Rocks Using Image Analysis and Effective Medium Theory

- ID: SCA2004-37, year: 2004
- Authors: M. Jurgawczynski, R. W. Zimmerman, X. D. Jing
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: permeability prediction, effective medium theory, image analysis, carbonate pore structure, hydraulic conductance, BSE micrographs
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2004/SCA2004-37.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2004-37)

Permeability is predicted from 2D pore image areas and perimeters using effective medium theory; applied unmodified to four carbonates, it works within a factor of two except for a vuggy sample.

Permeability prediction from 2D pore images, previously validated on sandstones, is applied to carbonates. Pore conductances are estimated from pore area and perimeter, with constriction and stereological corrections, and combined with Kirkpatrick effective medium theory assuming coordination number 6. Four carbonate samples (0.5-25 mD) were imaged by back-scattered electron micrographs without modifying the method. Three samples (A, E, F) were predicted within about a factor of two (e.g. 2.7 vs 2.5 mD, 12.5 vs 13 mD, 3.7 vs 1.9 mD). The vuggy, heterogeneous sample D was overpredicted by two orders of magnitude (3002 vs 25 mD), indicating the need for a way to identify and exclude unconnected vugs, e.g. by epoxy impregnation or statistical outlier detection.

## Angstrom to Millimeter Characterization of Rock Pore Structure and the Prediction of Conductivity and Permeability Thereof

- ID: SCA2004-42, year: 2004
- Authors: E.S. Amirtharaj, M.A. Ioannidis
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: mercury intrusion porosimetry, critical path analysis, pore-to-throat aspect ratio, formation factor, permeability prediction, Katz-Thompson
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2004/SCA2004-42.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2004-42)

Combining MIP and binary micrographs gives pore size and pore-to-throat aspect ratio, improving critical path predictions of formation factor and permeability for 14 sandstones and carbonates.

The paper applies a method that fuses mercury intrusion porosimetry with binary micrographs to obtain pore size distributions from 0.01 to 1000 µm. Comparing pore-size and throat-size volume distributions gives an apparent pore-to-throat aspect ratio for each pore size. A constricted-cylinder channel model is used in critical path analysis to derive new equations for permeability and formation factor. Tested on 14 sandstone and carbonate samples, the new permeability equation is off from measured values by a factor of 1.69 (±0.71), versus 1.86 (±0.72) for Katz-Thompson, which is not a significant improvement. The new formation factor equation is off by a factor of 1.70 (±0.71) versus 2.80 (±1.1) for Katz-Thompson, a significant improvement attributed to accounting for both pore and throat sizes. The aspect ratio may also indicate non-wetting phase trapping, as in Berea, where 37% residual mercury corresponds to pores with aspect ratio above 2.8.

## Porous Microstructure Characterization of a Sandstone Reservoir Using High-Resolution X-Ray Microtomography

- ID: SCA2005-41, year: 2005
- Authors: Carlos Roberto Appoloni, Carlos Rogério Oliveira Rodrigues, Celso Peres Fernandes
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: X-ray microtomography, pore size distribution, two-point correlation function, truncated Gaussian reconstruction, morphological opening, Botucatu sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2005/SCA2005-41.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2005-41)

A Botucatu sandstone was imaged by 3.8 µm µ-CT and compared with a truncated Gaussian 3D reconstruction, which underestimated large pores and long-range connectivity.

The paper characterizes the pore space of a Botucatu Formation eolian sandstone (30% porosity, 5,000 mD) using a Skyscan 1072 microfocus µ-CT with cone-beam reconstruction. A 3 mm by 5 mm sample gave 956 cross-sections at 3.8 µm resolution, validated against petrographic thin sections. Porosity, morphological pore size distribution and two-point autocorrelation were measured on 700 slices; average porosity was 30.2% versus 30.0% by gas expansion, and most pores were 10–55 µm with a peak near 30 µm. A truncated Gaussian 3D model (250^3 voxels, 30.26% porosity) built from the autocorrelation data matched the µ-CT roughly but missed pores above 100 µm and overestimated pores below 50 µm. Its effective porosity after removing isolated pores was 29.98%, showing the weakness in reproducing long-range connectivity even for a simple sandstone.

## Pore-Scale Heterogeneity Assessed by the Lattice-Boltzmann Method

- ID: SCA2006-44, year: 2006
- Authors: Hiroshi Okabe, Kazuhito Oseto
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: lattice-Boltzmann method, micro-CT, representative element volume, porosity-permeability correlation, pore-scale heterogeneity, digital rock
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-44.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-44)

Lattice-Boltzmann flow simulation on micro-CT images of Berea and Fontainebleau sandstones is used to estimate REV and show how pore-scale heterogeneity affects porosity–permeability relations.

The paper addresses how pore-scale heterogeneity can be assessed using digital rock images and numerical flow simulation. Micro-CT images (5.67 µm resolution) of Berea sandstone, a carbonate and Fontainebleau sandstone were binarized, and single-phase permeability was computed with a D3Q19 LBGK lattice-Boltzmann model with bounce-back walls and periodic boundaries. Subgrids were extracted from a 240³ cube (1.36 mm) of Berea; porosity and permeability variability decreased with increasing size, and cubes larger than about 1.2 mm appeared representative. The LBM gave a good φ-k correlation, and permeability was little affected by unresolved micro-porosity, although image porosity differed from the laboratory value. Artificial pore-dominant and throat-dominant structures showed higher permeability for throat-dominant geometry, and Fontainebleau subgrids at porosity near 0.14 showed varied permeability. Preliminary two-phase oil-water simulations illustrated the effect of pore aspect ratio on oil flow.

## A Pore Network Model to Calculate the Capillary Pressure and Relative Permeability Curves of Intermediate-Wet Reservoirs

- ID: SCA2006-45, year: 2006
- Authors: Christos D. Tsakiroglou
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network model, fractal roughness, intermediate wettability, contact angle, capillary pressure, relative permeability
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2006/SCA2006-45.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2006-45)

A pore-and-throat network with fractal wall roughness simulates drainage and imbibition across contact angles, showing nonlinear capillary pressure and relative permeability changes as wettability becomes intermediate.

The paper addresses the lack of a mechanistic model explaining the nonlinear variation of two-phase flow properties as a rock becomes intermediate-wet. A 3D network of spherical pores and cylindrical throats with fractal roughness (cones and triangular prisms) simulates quasistatic primary drainage and secondary imbibition, with contact angle varied from 0 to 180°. The network (permeability 443 mD, formation factor 12.5, total porosity 0.20, sharpness angle w=0.376 rad) has a critical contact angle of 68.4°. Above this angle, water in roughness is lost, the high-pressure part of the capillary pressure curve disappears and relative permeabilities change sharply. For imbibition, relative permeability curves group into families depending on water-wet versus intermediate-wet state rather than the exact contact angle.

## Pore Scale Modelling of Carbonate Reservoir Rocks

- ID: SCA2007_18, year: 2007
- Authors: Stig Bakke, Sven Roth, Rudolf J. Held, Håkon G. Rueslåtten
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: pore scale geomodel, carbonate dolostone, micro-CT, NMR T2 simulation, pore network extraction, process-based reconstruction
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_18.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_18)

A process-based reconstruction method extended from sandstones builds 3D dolostone pore-scale models at three resolutions; computed permeability, formation factor and NMR agree reasonably with micro-CT and plug data.

Carbonate diagenesis complicates pore-scale reconstruction, so the authors extended a process-based sandstone method to a dolomitized bioclastic peloidal grainstone. Models simulate sedimentation, grain reshaping, distance-transform-based replacive dolomite and micrite placement, calibrated to thin sections and synchrotron micro-CT at 8, 1.4 and 0.7 µm. Models matched micro-CT visually and by two-point correlation functions. Permeability, formation factor and NMR T2 were computed on models and CT images and compared with plug data (30.1% porosity, 1295 mD). The 8 µm model and CT gave about 2100–2500 mD, roughly 2 times above the plug, with an excellent model-CT match. The 1.4 and 0.7 µm models underestimated permeability by about 3.9 times, and their T2 peaks shifted to smaller pores because the volumes are below REV. Pore networks were extracted from all models (e.g., 72,487 nodes at 8 µm) for later multiphase flow simulation.

## The Use of Pore Scale Modeling to Predict Reservoir Parameters from Drill Cuttings

- ID: SCA2007_31, year: 2007
- Authors: Alex Mock, Pål-Eric Øren, Håkon Rueslåtten, Elin Rein, Sverre Henriksen
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: drill cuttings, pore-scale modeling, process-based reconstruction, lattice Boltzmann permeability, pore network modeling, NMR T2 simulation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_31.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_31)

Process-based 3D rock models built from drill cuttings (sieving, XRD, SEM, logs) yield computed permeability, NMR, elastic and two-phase properties; permeability agrees reasonably with measurements.

Cores are often unavailable, so the authors test whether drill cuttings can supply input for pore-scale modeling of reservoir properties. Four sandstone cuttings samples were studied, two from PDC bits (disintegrated grains mixed with mud) and two of conventional chips up to 1 mm. Grain size, mineralogy and porosity from sieving, XRD, SEM image analysis and porosity logs were used to reconstruct 3D models (4–7.6 mm cubes). Permeability, formation factor, elastic moduli, NMR T2, capillary pressure and relative permeability were computed. Calculated permeabilities generally fell within an order of magnitude of log or core values, with one exception, and sample 4 multiphase curves matched models from nearby core plugs. NMR, electrical and elastic results could not be validated, and laminations, vugs and sampling uncertainties limit the approach, which the authors call a pilot study.

## SEM Imaging of Dry and Saturated Porous Rocks for Modeling Fluid Distribution on Pore Scale

- ID: SCA2007_53, year: 2007
- Authors: Tania Hildebrand-Habel, George A. Virnovsky, Olav Inge Frette, Ingebret Fjelde
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: Cryo-SEM, pore-scale fluid distribution, epoxy impregnation, capillary bundle model, backscattered electron imaging, wetting films
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2007/SCA2007_53.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2007_53)

Tests SEM preparation methods and Cryo-SEM of oil/brine-saturated Bentheim sandstone and Liège chalk, comparing observed fluid distributions with capillary-bundle model predictions from SEM images.

The study seeks realistic pore-scale descriptions from SEM images to model fluid distribution. Dry Bentheim sandstone and Liège chalk were prepared by fracturing, grinding, thin sectioning and double epoxy impregnation with polishing; the last gave the best segmentable images at all magnifications. Images fed a capillary-bundle model (Virnovsky et al. 2006) to compute drainage capillary pressure and saturations, and high pixel density proved essential for chalk. Saturated plugs were frozen in liquid nitrogen and imaged by Cryo-SEM with BSE and X-ray maps. In sandstone, brine films and small water-filled pores were clearly seen and the model reproduced brine in small pores and oil in large ones, but not films. In chalk, micro- and nanopores prevented fluid distinction except in macropores.

## Does the Pore Class Concept for Carbonates Make Sense for Multiphase Flow?

- ID: SCA2008-29, year: 2008
- Authors: Shahram Pourmohammadi, Sverre Hetland, Kristine Spildo, Arne Skauge
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: carbonate pore classes, waterflood recovery, dispersivity, flowing fraction, formation resistivity factor, residual oil saturation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-29.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-29)

Waterflood recovery from 46 carbonate plugs is correlated with pore class and single-phase properties; flowing fraction at residual oil gives the most unique recovery trend.

The study tests whether carbonate pore classes (Lonoy scheme) help predict multiphase flow behavior. Forty-six plugs from the Barents Sea, North Sea and Middle East, spanning 9 pure and 2 mixed pore types, were waterflooded with synthetic seawater and decane at room temperature and 20 bar net overburden. Recovery was compared with formation factor, dispersivity and flowing fraction from earlier single-phase tests. Chalk gave high recovery (average about 69-73% OOIP), interparticle intermediate, mouldic low (about 36%), and intercrystalline scattered. Recovery correlated negatively with formation factor and dispersivity and positively with flowing fraction; flowing fraction at Sor gave a narrow trend independent of pore type.

## Pore Network Modelling on Carbonate: A Comparative Study of Different Micro-CT Network Extraction Methods

- ID: SCA2008-31, year: 2008
- Authors: Hu Dong, Ståle Fjeldstad, Luc Alberts, Sven Roth, Stig Bakke, Pål-Eric Øren
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: pore network extraction, medial axis, maximal ball algorithm, micro-CT carbonate, coordination number, network relative permeability
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-31.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-31)

Four skeleton extraction methods (medial axis, maximal ball, velocity based, grain recognition) are compared on sandstone and carbonate 3D images; maximal ball and velocity based perform best.

Pore network extraction from 3D images depends on how the topological skeleton is identified. Four methods (medial axis, maximal ball, flow velocity based, grain recognition) were applied to a sphere packing, two reconstructed Fontainebleau sandstones, two micro-CT carbonates and one micro-CT sandstone, using the same geometric partitioning afterwards. Networks were compared on coordination number, pore and throat size distributions, permeability, formation factor, and water-wet drainage and waterflood relative permeability. Medial axis and grain recognition gave more isolated and 1-connected pores; medial axis failed on the noisy, clay-bearing sandstone S1 (over 55% 0-connected pores). Maximal ball and velocity based networks over-predicted permeability by about 1.1 on average (medial axis 1.3, grain recognition 1.7), and all networks under-predicted formation factor by about 1.6. Drainage curves were similar across methods, and maximal ball and velocity based networks gave similar transport predictions.

## Investigation of Nano-Porosity in Carbonate Reservoirs and Its Role in Understanding Hydrocarbon Reserves and Recovery Economics

- ID: SCA2008-54, year: 2008
- Authors: Waleed Al-Bazzaz, Mohammad J. Ali, Misfera Al-Qahtani
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: nano-porosity, calcium silicate, mercury porosimetry, pore size distribution, tight carbonate, XRD
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2008/SCA2008-54.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2008-54)

XRD, SEM and mercury porosimetry show nano-pores hosted in calcium silicates of a tight Kuwaiti carbonate, holding up to 60% of porosity and extending hypothetical reservoir life.

A tight Kuwaiti carbonate reservoir has good core porosity (7-16%) but very low permeability (0.011-1.17 md). Four samples were examined using XRD, SEM/BSE with elemental mapping, and mercury porosimetry (Washburn equation, up to ~33,000 psia, to ~3 nm pore diameter). XRD identified calcium silicates such as wollastonite, a metamorphic product, as a major fabric component hosting nano-pores. Mercury intrusion indicated nano-porosity of about 60% of total porosity in plug 12 and about 20-25% in plug 24. A hypothetical 10 TCF case produced at 10,000 mcf/d gives a 5.1-year life for conventional pores only versus 12.74 years if nano-pores are included. Whether hydrocarbons reside in and can be produced from these pores was not tested, since no hydrocarbon analysis was available.

## Micromechanical Investigation of the Hydromechanical Behaviours of Carbonates Contribution of in-Situ Strain Field Measurement by Means of SEM and Optic Digital Image Correlation

- ID: SCA2009-22, year: 2009
- Authors: J. Dautriat, M. Bornert, N. Gland, A. Dimanov, J. Raphanel, O. Vizika
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: digital image correlation, Estaillades limestone, strain localization, in-situ SEM compression, microporous aggregates, microcracking
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2009/SCA2009-22.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2009-22)

Digital image correlation on optical and SEM images during compression of Estaillades limestone reveals strain localization controlled by dense and microporous aggregate distribution.

The paper investigates how microstructural heterogeneity of Estaillades limestone (bimodal porosity, dense and microporous calcite aggregates, ~28% porosity) governs deformation under uniaxial compression. Digital image correlation was applied to optical images at full-sample (~22 µm/pixel) and mesoscopic (~3.5 µm/pixel) scales on 50x100 mm cylinders, and to stepwise in-situ SEM compression of small parallelepipeds at X40 and X160. Optical DIC showed homogeneous strain up to ~0.1% axial strain, then a deformation lens and localization at the future fracture site (~0.37% strain, ~16 MPa), where dense aggregate clusters are concentrated. DIC-derived Young's modulus (~13 GPa) exceeded the LVDT value (~6 GPa) because of machine compliance. SEM DIC showed microcracks opening at interfaces of dense aggregates and in porous zones, while microporous aggregates accommodated strain diffusely; local strains exceeded 10% at crack openings.

## FIB/SEM and SEM/Edx: A New Dawn for the SEM in the Core Lab?

- ID: SCA2010-08, year: 2010
- Authors: P.W.S.K. Botha
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: FIB/SEM, gas shale, curtaining artifact, automated mineralogy, QEMSCAN, SEM/EDX
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2010/SCA2010-08.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2010-08)

Describes FIB/SEM nanoscale 3D pore imaging of gas shale, including curtaining artifacts, and SEM/EDX automated mineralogy applied to Mancos Shale cores, showing agreement with XRD.

Gas shale pore networks require nanometre-resolution 3D imaging, and mineralogy and lithotype are also key parameters. The paper explains FIB/SEM slice-and-view, in which ion milling alternates with SEM imaging to give a 3D model with nanometre XY resolution and 10 nm slice spacing. A shale example of 130 slices is shown, with segmentation difficulties because kerogen and pores have similar grey levels, and curtaining and charging artifacts are discussed. Automated SEM/EDX mineralogy (QEMSCAN) was applied to 27 Mancos Shale core samples with the USGS. Modal mineralogy compared well with RockJock XRD values and was used to reconstruct a mineralogic/stratigraphic sequence through the Bridge Creek and Fairport Limestone Members consistent with core logging. The authors conclude FIB/SEM is promising but representativeness remains open, and automated mineralogy is reliable and high throughput.

## Correlations Between SCAL Parameters and Porosity/Permeability: A Comparison Between Experimental Data and Simulated Data from a Pore Network Simulator

- ID: SCA2010-22, year: 2010
- Authors: A.W. Cense, R. Miskiewicz, R.M.M. Smits, X.D. Jing
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network modelling, Brooks-Corey drainage capillary pressure, Skjaeveland imbibition, Corey relative permeability, Amott-Harvey and USBM wettability, process-based reconstruction
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2010/SCA2010-22.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2010-22)

Correlations of fitted SCAL curve parameters with porosity and permeability, from in-house sandstone data and pore network simulations, agree for drainage but not for imbibition or wettability trends.

The study compares correlations between SCAL curve parameters and porosity/permeability/wettability from an in-house sandstone database with those from pore network simulations on virtual rocks. Experiments covered 443 samples for capillary pressure (drainage, imbibition, USBM and Amott-Harvey indices) and 74 samples from 13 reservoirs for relative permeability, fitted with Brooks-Corey, Skjaeveland and Corey forms. Thirty process-based rock models (Bentheimer, Berea, Fontainebleau, reservoir grain size distributions) were used in over 2600 eCore simulations with varying contact angles, oil-wet pore fractions and distributions. Drainage entry pressure, irreducible water saturation and shape factor from simulations fell within experimental error bars. Imbibition capillary pressure parameters disagreed, simulated points did not follow the Dixit mixed-wet-small/large/fractional classes in the IAH-IUSBM diagram, and relative permeability parameters gave inconsistent wettability inference (krw suggests intermediate-wet, nw water-wet, no oil-wet).

## Sandstone Sample Classification, Using High Resolution CT Method

- ID: SCA2010-34, year: 2010
- Authors: Dohnalik M, Zalewska J
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: micro-CT, pore structure classification, connected components, tortuosity, Rotliegend sandstone, finite element permeability simulation
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2010/SCA2010-34.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2010-34)

Micro-CT images of 12 Rotliegend sandstone samples are classified into three pore-structure types by connected-component volume classes; finite-element flow simulation on one crack overestimates permeability (7.1 vs 4.1 mD).

The study analyzes pore structure of eolian Rotliegend sandstones (porosity <13%, permeability <2 mD) from the North Sudeten Monocline using micro-CT at 5.8 µm voxel size. Binarized images were used to compute porosity, connected-component number and volume, tortuosity and connectivity. Connected components were grouped into six volume classes, and each sample's class participation was used to define three types: A (poor, 5 samples, CT porosity below ~3%), B (5 samples, intermediate) and C (2 samples, dominated by class VI, one with an open crack). CT porosity was well below mercury intrusion porosity because of resolution limits. Finite-element Navier-Stokes simulation on a downsampled crack from a type C sample gave 7.1 mD versus measured 4.1 mD (73% error), attributed to the 10x downsampling.

## Multiscale Pore System Reconstruction and Integration

- ID: SCA2011-36, year: 2011
- Authors: K. Wu, Z. Jiang, J. Ma, M.I.J. van Dijke, K.S. Sorbie
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore architecture models, multiscale pore network, stochastic network generation, connectivity function Euler number, carbonate, network modelling
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-36.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-36)

Coarse- and fine-scale 3D carbonate reconstructions from 2D thin sections are merged into stochastic multiscale pore networks, whose predicted permeability, capillary pressure and relative permeability match lab data.

Single-resolution images cannot capture the many length scales of carbonate pore systems. The authors reconstruct 3D coarse and fine models from 2D SEM thin-section images (resolutions differing by about 4x) using Markov chain Monte Carlo pore architecture models, then extract a network from each. They generate stochastic equivalent networks and integrate them into a nested multiscale network, using a connectivity function (specific Euler number) to guide how fine bonds connect coarse and fine nodes. For two carbonates, integrated-network permeability rose well above that of the coarse or fine networks alone (e.g. 109 mD vs lab 103 mD for Case 1; 1.5 mD vs 1.9 mD for Case 2). Simulated mercury injection and imbibition relative permeability for Case 1 agreed well with lab data, showing that small-scale pores connecting isolated vugs control the effective properties.

## Extraction of the Porosity and Permeability for Small Plugs

- ID: SCA2011-45, year: 2011
- Authors: Jianlei Sun, Shameem Siddiqui, M. Rafiqul
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: CT heterogeneity index, cutting-size samples, resin encasing, steady-state relative permeability, thin section porosity, Berea sandstone
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 1
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-45.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-45)

Compares porosity, permeability, relative permeability and CT-based heterogeneity of six core plugs against resin-encased cutting-size subsamples, finding good agreement for Berea but poorer for heterogeneous carbonates.

The study tests whether petrophysical data from large cutting-size samples (14-21 mm long, about 9 mm diameter) represent their parent core plugs. Six plugs (three dolomite, one limestone, two Berea sandstone) were measured for porosity, absolute and unsteady-state relative permeability, CT scanned, and thin sectioned; seventeen subsamples were drilled axially and radially and embedded in resin disks. A composite heterogeneity index (CHI) combining intra-slice CV and inter-slice index from CT numbers ranked the samples identically at both scales, with the limestone most heterogeneous. Porosity and permeability matched well for Berea but poorly for the limestone and dolomites. Thin-section ImageJ porosities (e.g. 0.155, 0.148, 0.12, 0.15) were close to plug values (0.157, 0.152, 0.168, 0.157). CT-assisted steady-state relative permeability on a Berea subsample differed in magnitude and shape from the plug's JBN unsteady-state curves.

## Small Angle Neutron Scattering Analysis of Porous Reservoir Rocks

- ID: SCA2011-48, year: 2011
- Authors: T. Drobek, J. Strobel, S. Park, K. L. Phuong, W. Altermann, H. Lemmel, P. Lindner, R.W. Stark
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: small angle neutron scattering, USANS, fractal dimension, Rotliegend sandstone, mercury injection capillary pressure, permeability
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-48.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-48)

SANS and USANS on Rotliegend and Bentheim sandstones give pore-structure fractal dimensions that agree with petrophysical estimates and explain mercury capillary pressure behavior.

Permeability in the Völkersen gas field varies widely at a given porosity (0.1 to 200 mD at 12%), and fractal pore-structure models have so far relied on computed rather than measured fractal dimensions. Thin slices (0.5-4 mm) of five Rotliegend plugs and one Bentheim sandstone were measured by SANS (D11) and USANS (S18) at the ILL. Power-law fits of intensity versus scattering vector Q gave fractal dimensions D that decrease with increasing porosity and permeability. Large features gave D around 2.2, while small features gave D up to 3. USANS-derived D agreed well with values calculated from porosity, permeability and formation factor. Mercury injection curves showed that low D from USANS controls low-pressure behavior, while high-pressure behavior needs the higher SANS D, so radii from capillary pressure are apparent values that include roughness.

## The Impact of Heterogeneity and Multi-Scale Measurements on Reservoir Characterization and Stooip Estimations

- ID: SCA2011-49, year: 2011
- Authors: Moustafa Dernaika, Samy Serag, M. Zubair, Abu Dhabi
- Method: laboratory experiment
- Keywords: whole core analysis, multi-scale heterogeneity, carbonate porosity and permeability, porous plate capillary pressure, saturation exponent n, STOOIP uncertainty
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-49.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-49)

Compares porosity, permeability, anisotropy, capillary pressure and resistivity index across 4-inch whole cores and 1.5-inch and 1-inch plugs from Abu Dhabi carbonate wells, finding scale-dependent heterogeneity effects.

Reservoir heterogeneity in a giant Abu Dhabi carbonate field was examined by comparing properties measured at several sample scales, extending earlier work on one well. Over 200 four-inch whole cores from four wells were measured, with 1.5-inch plugs and 1-inch trims cut from them for direct comparison, including poroperm, MICP, porous-plate Pc and resistivity index. About 70% of whole cores gave lower porosity than 1.5-inch plugs (average 2 p.u., maximum 9 p.u.), and the 1-inch comparison argues against a surface/volume explanation. Whole core permeability tended to be higher overall, though plugs gave higher values at high permeability, and plug Kv/Kh data scattered more than whole core data. Preliminary whole core Pc and RI curves resembled plug curves, with plugs reaching lower water saturation at the same Pc step and a marginally higher n in the whole core; these tests are still ongoing.

## Rock Typing and Permeability Prediction for Water-Wet and Oil-Wet Rocks

- ID: SCA2011-53, year: 2011
- Authors: Pudji Permadi
- Method: data analysis or correlation
- Keywords: rock typing, permeability prediction, irreducible water saturation, wettability, pore geometry-pore structure plot, capillary tube model
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-53.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-53)

A capillary-tube-based pore geometry vs pore structure cross-plot defines rock types and permeability correlations for water-wet carbonate and oil-wet sandstone, outperforming Timur, Tixier, Coates and Torskaya correlations.

The paper develops a rock typing method from a capillary tube model, cross-plotting pore geometry (k/φ) against pore structure (k/φ³) on a log-log PGS plot. Core plugs from a water-wet carbonate (four rock types) and an oil-wet sandstone (three rock types) were grouped by microscopic geological features, and the groups formed separate clusters on the PGS plot with slopes below 0.5. Combining each rock type equation with a Swirr–k power law gives a Timur-like permeability correlation, k = const·φ^p/Swirr^q, with rock-type-specific exponents. Porosity exponent p decreases with rock quality (p < 2) in both rocks, while the Swirr exponent decreases with quality in the carbonate but increases in the oil-wet sandstone, indicating a wettability effect. The new correlations gave r² of 0.99 (carbonate) and 0.96 (sandstone) with lower relative errors than Tixier, Timur, Coates and Torskaya.

## Mineral Distribution in Reservoir Rocks and Its Impact on Enhanced Oil Recovery

- ID: SCA2011-57, year: 2011
- Authors: James J. Howard, Gary L. Lovell
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: SEM-EDS mineral mapping, low-salinity waterflooding, pore-adjacent mineralogy, clay minerals, XRD comparison, glauconite
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2011/SCA2011-57.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2011-57)

SEM-EDS mineral mapping matches XRD bulk mineralogy and shows that clay minerals adjacent to pores, not total clay, correlate with low-salinity waterflood oil recovery.

The paper addresses how minerals are distributed relative to pores, since fluid-rock interactions occur at pore walls. Sandstone sets A, B and C were imaged by SEM with automated EDS mineral mapping (MLA), with epoxy-filled pores, BSE to separate void from mineral, and EDS spectra on a grid matched to end-member standards. Bulk mineralogy agreed well with Rietveld XRD for sets B and C, while early set A results overstated clays because of coarse grid, low magnification and too many standards. Nearest-neighbor analysis gave the mineralogy adjacent to pores, for example illite coating larger pore walls in set C. In set A low-salinity tertiary flood response did not correlate with total clay but trended with the fraction of pore surface in contact with clay (glauconite), and set B showed a similar positive correlation.

## SCA2012-01

- ID: SCA2012-01, year: 2012
- Authors: Bondino I, Hamon G, W. Kallel, Kachuma D
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: pore network modelling, network extraction, Lattice-Boltzmann, relative permeability prediction, wettability, micro-CT
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 6
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-01.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-01)

Simulations with pore network models and Lattice-Boltzmann show that network extraction, rock representation and wettability, not pore-filling physics, limit prediction of two-phase relative permeability from digital rocks.

The paper tests whether digital rock technology can genuinely predict two-phase relative permeability, extending the critique of Sorbie and Skauge. Networks were extracted from micro-CT images of Clashach sandstone (about 1000 mD, 17% porosity) using different algorithms, and Kr was simulated in water-wet and oil-wet conditions. Changing only the throat length definition on the same image altered Kr more than changing the sample, and different networks gave conventional, intermediate or "counterintuitive" wettability trends. A simple lattice showed that a pore volume exponent of 1 yields the counterintuitive trend while 2 gives the expected one. Geometric versus thermodynamic layer-collapse models differed by under 5% in residual oil on a Berea network, but contact angle correlation had a larger effect. Lattice-Boltzmann and capillary drainage transform results on Clashach gave expected wettability trends, but image-based Kro bore no resemblance to network-based Kro.

## Numerical Pore-Network Fusion to Predict Capillary Pressure and Relative Permeability in Carbonates

- ID: SCA2012-02, year: 2012
- Authors: Sven Roth, Danyong Li, Hu Dong, Martin J. Blunt
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: pore network fusion, dual porosity carbonate, micro-CT and nano-CT imaging, stochastic network generation, capillary pressure, relative permeability
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-02.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-02)

Pore networks from 1.2 µm micro-CT and 65 nm nano-CT images of a vuggy carbonate are fused into a dual-porosity network, giving more realistic flow properties than either alone.

Vuggy, micrite-rich carbonates cannot be captured in a single 3D image because of the trade-off between resolution and volume. A vuggy carbonate was imaged at 1.2 µm (micro-CT) and 65 nm (nano-CT), and maximal-ball pore networks were extracted from both. A stochastic equivalent of the fine network was generated and fused into the coarse network, with cross-connections made only by fine throats. The combined network (~425,000 elements, 480 µm side) has porosity 19.5%, permeability 1.2 mD, formation factor ~21 and cementation exponent 1.86, versus 0.7 mD and FF 2524 (m=3.46) for the poorly connected coarse network and 0.28 mD, FF 72 (m=2.2) for the nano network. Oil-wet two-phase simulations (70% oil-wet pores) showed the coarse network gave almost no two-phase flow. The nano network alone missed the capillary pressure behavior over the full saturation range, and the combined network gave lower Swi (0.02) and a lower crossover saturation. Some plateaus in the relative permeability curves are finite-size artifacts of unknown origin.

## Quantifying Reactive Flow-Induced Pore Evolutions in Microporous Carbonate Cores Using X-Ray Micro-Computed Tomography

- ID: SCA2012-09, year: 2012
- Authors: Jafar Qajar, Nicolas Francois, Christoph H. Arns
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: micro-CT image registration, reactive flow, carbonate dissolution, three-phase segmentation, microporosity, fines migration
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-09.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-09)

Registered pre- and post-dissolution μ-CT images, three-phase segmentation and 2D intensity histograms quantify dissolution and fines migration in two EDTA-flooded limestones, including microporosity effects.

Acidizing changes carbonate pore structure and can release fines, but microporosity has made pore-scale quantification difficult. EDTA (0.25 M, pH 12) was injected into Savonniere (58.6 md, 30.6% porosity) and Shuaiba (3.2 md, 21% porosity) limestone plugs, with μ-CT imaged before and after at ~4.4 μm voxels and registered in 3D. Three-phase segmentation with microporosity assignment and 2D voxel intensity histograms defined 12 evolution scenarios grouped into dissolution, particle displacement, unaltered pore and unaltered mineral regions. Savonniere showed quasi-uniform dissolution, whereas Shuaiba formed wormholes. Image-derived net dissolution ratio for Savonniere (18.3%) matched the lab value (17.3%). Permeability first declined from fines clogging, then rose. Ignoring microporosity in two-phase segmentation underestimated dissolution and particle displacement fractions considerably.

## Micron to Millimeter Upscaling of Shale Rock Properties Based on 3D Imaging and Modeling

- ID: SCA2012-20, year: 2012
- Authors: Shuang Zhang, Robert E. Klimentidis, Patrick Barthelemy
- Method: digital rock or imaging
- Keywords: FIB-SEM, micro-CT, shale permeability tensor, Stokes flow, Darcy-scale upscaling, digital rock physics
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 2
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-20.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-20)

Proposes a workflow upscaling shale permeability and porosity from FIB-SEM pore-scale Stokes simulations to Darcy-scale CT models of a fragment, demonstrated as a feasibility study.

Shale pore structure spans nanometers to millimeters, making upscaling of 3D image data difficult. A Devonian shale fragment was scanned by CT (25 µm voxels), and heterogeneities H1 (large voids), H2 (organic-rich) and H3 (mineral-rich) were identified. Small H2 and H3 regions were imaged by FIB-SEM (20 nm voxels) and Stokes flow gave permeability tensors: H2 about 37-57 µD with 37.7% porosity, H3 about 66-216 nD with 4.57% porosity. These properties were assigned to the segmented CT volume in Darcy-scale DuMux simulations. Single-phase runs on homogenized models matched the pore-scale pressure drop for one zone, and five zones captured the trend with some deviation. Including H2/H3 gave pressure extremes outside the imposed range, and a two-phase water-oil displacement was demonstrated. Validation against physical experiments, grid convergence and representative volume remain to be done.

## Quantitative analysis of kerogen content and mineralogy in shale cuttings by Diffuse Reflectance Infrared Fourier Transform Spectroscopy

- ID: SCA2012-27, year: 2012
- Authors: Alyssa Charsky, Michael M. Herron
- Method: measurement technique or apparatus
- Keywords: DRIFTS, Kubelka-Munk, kerogen quantification, shale cuttings mineralogy, least squares spectral fitting, DRFT-IR
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-27.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-27)

DRIFTS spectra of cleaned, ground shale cuttings, fitted by least squares to mineral and kerogen standards, give mineralogy within about 2 wt% and kerogen within 0.7 wt%.

Quantitative DRIFTS analysis has historically been unreliable because of non-linearity from particle size and orientation effects. The authors removed oil-based mud from cuttings, reduced particle size, converted spectra to Kubelka-Munk units and solved by least squares against a library of mineral and kerogen standards. Validation used 70 binary artificial mixtures (average absolute differences 1.9-4.1 wt%) and 95 Texas tight shale cuttings compared with DRFT-IR mineralogy and LECO TOC×1.2. For cuttings, average absolute differences were 2.2 (carbonate), 2.7 (clay), 2.5 (quartz), 1.0 (kaolinite) and 0.7 wt% (kerogen). Kerogen standards need calibration on about 20 samples per basin because kerogen maturity changes the C-H response.

## Effects of Wettability Alternation Simulation by Lattice Boltzmann in Porous Media

- ID: SCA2012-52, year: 2012
- Authors: Ping Chen, Guo Tao, Mingzhe Dong, Yihua Zhu
- Method: numerical simulation
- Keywords: lattice Boltzmann method, Shan-Chen pseudo-potential model, wettability, mixed-wet porous media, water flooding, relative permeability
- Cited by 0 papers in the corpus, cites 0
- [PDF](https://www.jgmaas.com/SCA/2012/SCA2012-52.pdf) · [On the site](https://mralbu.github.io/sca-papers/#SCA2012-52)

2D Shan-Chen lattice Boltzmann simulations show how external force and wettability type (water-, oil-, intermediate-, mixed-wet) control water flooding displacement and oil trapping in porous models.

The paper uses a 2D Shan-Chen pseudo-potential lattice Boltzmann model to study how wettability affects two-phase flow at the pore scale. Droplet simulations in periodic lattices (100x200, 100x100) show that increasing external force raises droplet height, i.e. increases non-wetting behavior and can reverse wettability. Water flooding in synthetic grain-pack models gives the highest oil displacement and oil relative permeability for intermediate-wet, then water-wet, then oil-wet, where water fingers through large pores and traps oil in small pores. In mixed-wet models, flooding is more efficient and the front more stable when small pores are oil-wet (MWS) than when large pores are oil-wet (MWL), where oil is trapped in small pores. The results are qualitative numerical simulations without experimental validation.
